MetaCheck Report

Just the Facts: How Dialogues with AI Reduce Conspiracy Beliefs

To fix (4)

To fix Ethics Approval Check

What we found

1 of 1 paper appeared to involve live data collection and lacked an ethics approval statement.

Show details

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, but it was not present:

Other conditions hold that content constant while varying how the AI or the interaction is framed to participants: Overt, in which participants are told of the AI’s persuasive goal, and Adversarial, in which the exchange is framed as a debate.

Study 2 also included a Control condition wherein participants completed an AI conversation on a topic unrelated to conspiracy beliefs.

To reduce the chance that results would depend on any one arbitrary control topic, control participants were randomly assigned to one of three unrelated conversation topics (experiences with the American medical system, views about firefighters, or cats versus dogs as companions).

Study 1 was recruited from CloudResearch in two waves, each advertised as a 17.5minute study and run under one protocol, apart from two sentences of the No Facts prompt (SI S6.2; raw N = 3,988; 2,585 in Sample 1a and 1,403 in Sample 1b).

Study 2 was recruited from CloudResearch in a single wave under the same intake protocol as Study 1 (raw N = 2,082); the preregistered target was 400 conspiracy believers in each treatment arm and 200 in Control (the sensitivity of the achieved sample is reported with the Study 2 results).

Participants were U.S. adults recruited through CloudResearch Connect who completed the study in English; all descriptive statistics refer to the final analytic samples.

In Study 1, conspiracy-believing participants were more Republicanleaning (party preference d = 0.35; 39% vs.

80.5; difference = 2.03, 95% CI [-0.95, 5.01], p = .18), and participants were no more likely to flag the shorter statement as an inaccurate summary of their belief (3.6% vs.

Participants are the Study 1 analytic sample excluding No Facts, restricted to the 1,058 with a non-empty explanation and cached codes (codes exist for 1,061 of 1,161 eligible participants; three blank responses were excluded).

Study 1 ran in two waves from the same participant pool under one protocol, apart from two sentences of the No Facts prompt (Samples 1a and 1b; §S6.2).

After giving consent, participants completed a CAPTCHA; those who failed it or opened the survey on a mobile device were screened out ( §S2.1).

Participants were then randomly assigned to a condition (Table S1; a ninth, alternative-model arm in Sample 1a is excluded, §S2.1).

In the Abstracted Summary condition, the summarization prompt also told the AI not to mention any specific evidence the participant had given ( §S1.3.1).

Participants were randomly assigned to one of three conditions, all run with GPT-5.2 (gpt-5.2-2025-12-11).

In Control, participants had a conversation of comparable format on one of three unrelated topics, assigned at random: the American medical system, firefighters, or cats versus dogs.

Participants were excluded if they (a) failed the attention check (one item in Study 1, two in Study 2; §S1.3.5), (b) failed the writing screener ( §S1.3.3), which ran inside the survey before the experiment and so has no step in Figures S1-S2, (c) did not articulate a conspiracy belief according to the classifier ( §S1.3.2), or (d) rated their pretreatment belief below 50.

Another 75 retained records are a participant’s second submission whose first had been excluded earlier in the cascade (58 of these participants had already talked with the AI in the alternative-model arm or the non-conspiracy branch).

The analysis in the main text (Figure 3C) uses Study 1 of Costello et al. (2024), whose participants completed the six-item AOT subscale (1-6, all items reverse-scored; α = .91, M = 4.11, SD = 1.05) at a follow-up a median of 12.0 days after the experiment (IQR 11.8-12.3).

The bracketed fields were filled with the Table S31 definitions, the attribute being coded, the question the participant had answered, and whether their belief had fallen (“succeeded”) or not (“failed”); the condition was not given.

power_id text
1 Study 1 was recruited from CloudResearch in two waves, each advertised as a 17.5minute study and run under one protocol, apart from two sentences of the No Facts prompt (SI S6.2; raw N = 3,988; 2,585 in Sample 1a and 1,403 in Sample 1b). The first wave (Sample 1a) was the original preregistered launch. Our preregistered allocation in Sample 1a was n = 200 per active condition and n = 400 in the Baseline treatment (more in the Baseline treatment to increase between-group precision). The preregistration listed ten conditions; two open-access model-comparison arms (Llama 3 70B and Llama 2 13B), together with an exploratory GPT-3.5 arm, were run only as a Sample 1a pilot (n = 173 after screening) and are excluded from all analyses (Figure S1). After Sample 1a closed, the rate at which open-ended responses were classified as a conspiracy belief was lower than our preregistration assumed, so we ran a second wave (Sample 1b). Sample 1b used weighted randomization that, after pooling across waves, brought each of the eight remaining conditions close to its target sample size; sample size was indexed to a power calculation on Sample 1a effect sizes, which found that n = 150 per active arm and n = 350 in the Baseline treatment gave 80% power to detect a halving of treatment efficacy relative to the Baseline treatment. The exclusions, applied in order, were: failed CAPTCHA (n = 8), accessed the survey on a mobile device (n = 20), did not reach or failed the embedded attention check (n = 297), did not reach the AI treatment (n = 163), assigned to the pilot model-comparison arms (n = 173), did not articulate a conspiracy belief (n = 1,828), pretreatment belief below 50/100 (n = 96), missing post-treatment belief (attrition; n = 38), and cross-wave duplicates dropped at the participant ID level (n = 75). The full cascade is shown in Figure S1. The final analytic sample was N = 1,290 (Sample 1a: 806; Sample 1b: 484). Percondition ns after pooling were Baseline treatment = 333, Abstracted Summary = 164, Belief-First Rating = 137, Concise = 137, Adversarial = 131, No Facts = 129, Overt = 132, Just-the-Facts = 127.
2 Five Study 1 conditions were designed as targeted ablations of the Baseline treatment, and two altered the measurement procedure (Table 1). We compared the post-treatment belief score of each condition against the Baseline treatment while controlling for pre-treatment belief, and we report contrasts as differences in belief reduction, so that positive values indicate a larger reduction than in the Baseline treatment. In addition to the preregistered linear regressions, we also conducted complementary post hoc Bayesian regressions (Figure 1A). Given that many of the results are nulls, in the main text we focus on the Bayesian analyses as they quantify evidence for the null-difference interpretation rather than merely failure to reject it (full frequentist statistics for all contrasts are provided in Supplemental Tables S6 and S8, and are qualitatively equivalent). These contrasts could detect only large differences: each null contrast had 80% power for a difference of about 6-7 points, that is, 53-63% of the Baseline effect.

Baseline effect: Study 1, the raw Baseline pre-post reduction; Study 2, the HC3 Baseline-vs-Control contrast. Lower 95% bound as % = lower 95% limit of the difference divided by the Baseline effect, i.e., the largest loss compatible with the data. Retained share = (Baseline effect + difference)/Baseline effect, per posterior draw (Study 2: (Single Message -Control)/(Baseline treatment -Control)). Non-inferiority p: one-sided HC3 test against a loss of half the Baseline effect (margin chosen post hoc), Holm-adjusted in parentheses across the six Study 1 variants other than No Facts. 11 Participants in Study 2 likewise entered with strong endorsement of their focal conspiracy theories (pretreatment M = 80.7). In the Baseline treatment, belief decreased from 80.5 to 65.9, a mean reduction of 14.6 points, replicating Study 1, whereas the Control condition showed only a 1.4-point mean reduction (posterior mean difference from the Baseline treatment = -13.11, 95% CrI [-16.52, -9.71], BF10 > 1,000; Baseline treatment vs. Control d ppc = 0.88, 95% CI [0.69, 1.07], p < .001; Figure 1B); the Baseline effect is therefore not attributable to regression to the mean. Among Baseline-treatment participants who began above the scale midpoint, 23.1% (75 of 324) ended below it, compared with 1.5% (3 of 197) in Control (Fisher’s exact p < .001; Supplemental Table S9). Study 2 then asked whether the dialogic format itself was critical. As in Study 1, each condition was compared with the Baseline treatment, controlling for pretreatment belief, with the Baseline treatment as the reference group (Figure 1B; Table S8). The Single Message produced a large reduction relative to Control (posterior mean difference = 10.28, 95% CrI [6.93, 13.65], BF10 > 1,000; Single Message vs. Control d ppc = 0.69, 95% CI [0.53, 0.87], p < .001), with a raw decrease of 11.76 points and 16.8% of participants who began above the midpoint ending below it. Compared with the Baseline treatment, the Single Message reduced belief by slightly less (posterior mean difference = -2.82, 95% CrI [-5.71, 0.06], BF01 = 2.1; HC3 b = 2.87 on the post-treatment belief scale, 95% CI [-0.36, 6.10], p = .081; d ppc = -0.19, 95% CI [-0.41, 0.02]), retaining an estimated 79% of the Baseline effect (95% CrI [58%, 101%]); this contrast had 80% power to detect a difference of about 4.6 points (35% of the Baseline effect). Thus, even when the treatment was reduced to a single tailored AI responsewith no iterative give-and-take, no ongoing rapport-building, and no dynamic responding -it produced a large reduction in conspiracy belief. Multi-turn dialogue is therefore not necessary to obtain a large effect, although it may confer some additional benefit beyond the static message.
12 Each Study 1 null contrast could detect only large differences: with 80% power, 5.8-6.8 points, or 53-63% of the Baseline treatment’s 10.88-point reduction; power to detect a halving of the effect (5.44 points) was .61 to .75. Table S7 reports what the six variants that did not differ from Baseline rule out. All six pass a one-sided non-inferiority test against a half-effect margin (Holm-adjusted p < .05), and the posterior probability that each retains at least half the Baseline effect is ≥ .97. Only for Overt does the 95% interval include a loss of more than half the effect. Two-sided equivalence tests at the same margin fail only for Concise (p = .13) and Abstracted Summary (p = .07), whose estimates lie above Baseline. On average the six variants differ from Baseline by 0.62 points (HC3 95% CI [-2.07, 3.31]). The half-effect margin is lenient, and because Study 1 has no untreated control, the shares are relative to pre-post change, which includes roughly 1.4-1.5 points of untreated drift (Study 2 Control).

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Marginal Significance

What we found

You described 1 effect with terms related to ‘marginally significant’.

Show details

You described effects with terms related to ‘marginally significant’. If p values above 0.05 are interpreted as an effect, you inflate the alpha level, and increase the Type 1 error rate. If a p value is higher than the prespecified alpha level, it should be interpreted as a non-significant result.

Text Section Header
Neither interaction with the composite approached significance (Table S15). S5.4 Prior Exposure to Counterarguments (Study 2)

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate at which non-significant p-values are interpreted as marginally significant, see:

Olsson-Collentine A, van Assen MALM, Hartgerink CHJ (2019). “The Prevalence of Marginally Significant Results in Psychology Over Time.” Psychological Science, 30, 576–586. doi:10.1177/0956797619830326.

For the list of terms used to identifify marginally significant results, see this blog post by Matthew Hankins.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 10 imprecise p values out of 172 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (2)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 76 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

To check Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests

What we found

We found 28 t-tests and/or F-tests where effect sizes are not reported. Check these tests in the table below, and consider adding effect sizes.

Show details

We recommend checking the sentences below, and add any missing effect sizes.

For t-tests with a reported d, coherence checks yielded 1 indeterminate case.
Fit within each experiment, the interaction ranged from 1.17 (present Study 2) to 3.01 (Costello et al., 2024, Study 1) and did not differ across experiments (F(2, 2581) = 1.15, p = .317; Table S12).

Of the present experiments, only Study 2 can test whether the treatment effect itself differs by topic, because Study 1 has no control arm; there, the randomized Baseline treatment × topic test is not significant (p = .274; §S5.5, Table S18b), as in Costello et al. (2024; F(12, 1971) = 1.30, p = .21).

title LLM Persuasion Experiment (Extension #1 of Costello et al. 2024) Conspiracy mechanism s2
date_created 2024/05/08 12:26 (PT) 2026/01/27 11:53 (PT)
date_registered 2024-05-08 2026-01-27
existing_data_explanation No, no data have been collected for this study yet. No, no data have been collected for this study yet.
research_questions This study examines how various factors impact the ability of large language models to reduce belief in conspiracies, including (1) different AI model prompts, (2) different AI models (including older versions and non-OpenAI models), (3) changes to the pre- and post-treatment measurement materials, (4) changes to AI summarization of conspiracy beliefs, and (5) varying instructions for the human research subjects. This study asks how much of the previously documented ability of LLM dialogues to reduce conspiracy belief arises from the LLM having a back-and-forth dialogue, versus just sending a single message. We also compare the baseline debunking effect an active control condition.
design_dependent_variables Post-treatment belief in participants’ focal conspiracy (controlling for pre-treatment belief) will be the key dependent variable. Post-treatment belief in participants’ focal conspiracy (controlling for pre-treatment belief) will be the key dependent variable.
study_design_overview Participants will enter either a baseline condition or one of 9 experimental conditions. No conditions will be crossed.

Prompt Variations:

Condition 1: Instructing GPT-4 Turbo to persuade without using reason-based strategies.

Condition 2: Instructing GPT-4 Turbo to provide accurate information about the conspiracy belief, but not otherwise attempt to persuade the participant.

Condition 3: Curtailing the length of GPT-4’s responses

Model Comparisons:

Condition 4: Using LLama 3 70B rather than GPT-4 Turbo

Condition 5: LLama 2 13b rather than GPT-4 Turbo

Pre- and Post-Treatment Measures:

Condition 6: Asking participants to indicate their level of belief in the chosen conspiracy before asking them to provide evidence supporting their belief.

AI Summarization Changes:

Condition 7: When the AI is tasked with summarizing participants’ conspiracy beliefs – so as to provide a person-specific measurement occasion – it will exclude any specific evidence from the summary.

Instructions for Human Subjects:

Condition 8: Telling participants that the AI will be trying to persuade them prior to the dialogues.

Condition 9: Telling participants that they will be debating with the AI, which is trying to persuade them against their conspiracy belief, prior to the dialogues. 		Participants will be randomized into a control condition (discuss irrelevant topic with LLM), a static debunking message, or a debunking conversation with the LLM.
statistical_tests We will conduct a series of linear regression analyses comparing each experimental condition against the baseline condition using the following model: Post-treatment belief ~ [Dummy coded variable for baseline vs. condition] + pre-treatment belief). Statistical inference will be drawn using two-tailed t-tests for the Condition coefficient. We will also compare changes in model fit (i.e., with the addition of the Condition dummy-coded variables into the model) using R-squared values and Bayesian Information Criterion. The alpha level for the addition of the dummy-coded variables into the model will be set at 0.05. We will conduct this linear regression:

Post-treatment belief ~ control_dummy + static_dummy + pre-treatment belief. Statistical inference will be drawn using two-tailed t-tests for the condition dummies.
outliers_and_exclusions Participants who provide an initial belief rating below 50% certainty for their focal conspiracy theory will be excluded.
Participants who do not report believing in a conspiracy theory will be excluded (using the procedures in Costello et al. [2024]).
Participants who do not pass the initial writing quality screener will be excluded.
Participants who fail an attention check prior to treatment will be excluded. 		Participants who provide an initial belief rating below 50% certainty for their focal conspiracy theory will be excluded.

sample_size Each condition will have a target sample size of 200 participants, except for the baseline condition which will aim for 400 participants to provide a comprehensive comparison group.
Total planned sample size is thus 2,200 participants. 		We will aim to recruit 400 conspiracy believers for each treatment and 200 conspiracy believers for the control.

How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

:::

Full text

Abstract

Personalized dialogues with AI substantially reduce conspiracy beliefs. To identify why, we conducted two experiments with U.S. adults recruited online (total N = 2,193) that removed possible causes one at a time. The debunking effect was robust to disclosing the AI's persuasive goal, framing the exchange as a debate, instructing the AI to inform rather than persuade, halving its response length, altering how beliefs were measured, and replacing the dialogue with a single tailored message. Only instructing the AI to persuade without factual evidence and arguments substantially reduced the debunking effect. Content analyses of the AI's output and participants' own accounts linked larger reductions in conspiracy beliefs to factual argumentation rather than rapport, storytelling, or appeals to empathy. AI dialogues appear to reduce conspiracy beliefs primarily by supplying relevant, well-reasoned counterevidence. Our results suggest that humans might be similarly effective if they could supply comparable evidence.

[div-01]

Conspiracy theories -explanations of important events as secret, malevolent plots by powerful actors -are widespread and can have serious consequences for public health, democratic legitimacy, extremism, and scientific progress (Ecker et al., 2024). Although some conspiracies are real, many popular conspiracy beliefs are clearly contradicted by evidence but are nonetheless hard to correct. In meta-analyses, corrections of conspiracy theories and related misinformation have reliable but typically modest effects that often shrink or vanish for highly committed believers or ideologically resonant claims (Albarracin & Shavitt, 2018; Chan & Albarracín, 2023; Walter et al., 2020). Lay and academic accounts have therefore often assumed that no brief intervention, least of all one that simply presents counterevidence, can durably move such beliefs (Lewandowsky et al., 2013; O'Mahony et al., 2023; Sunstein & Vermeule, 2009), which are seen as sustained by motivated reasoning, distrust of official sources, and selfsealing explanation (Biddlestone et al., 2021; Dagnall et al., 2015; Douglas et al., 2024) or as socially adaptive commitments or group-signaling rationalizations rather than attempts to track truth (Mercier, 2020; Williams, 2023).

However, recent evidence indicates that personalized dialogues with AI models can meaningfully reduce conspiracy beliefs (Costello et al., 2024). In these studies, a substantial fraction of believers revised their beliefs after sustained, belief-relevant counterargumentation, with unusually large effects (ds of 0.79 and 1.15). This finding may complicate, or even run counter to, portraits of conspiracy believers as insensitive to evidence because they are motivated by needs other than accuracy. What, exactly, underpins the AI's outsized effect?

One account is content-based. In classical reasoning and Bayesian accounts of belief updating, sufficiently diagnostic counterevidence should induce deliberation and subsequent belief revision (Pennycook, 2023). As such, the treatment would succeed by presenting relevant counterevidence, reasoning, and alternative explanations that directly address participants' stated reasons for believing. Generative AI may be distinctive in delivering expert-level knowledge across remarkably broad domains (Farrell et al., 2025; Singhal et al., 2025), whereas any human debunker, however well informed, commands only a small fraction of the counterevidence relevant to the conspiracies participants might name and so cannot reliably tailor arguments to each believer's claims and reasoning (Costello, 2025; Williams, 2026); the AI's advantage would lie in its informational facility.

Another class of accounts is source-based: participants defer to the AI because of what they think it is. AI systems may be seen as more objective, neutral, accurate, or less politically motivated than human interlocutors, consistent with work on source credibility and "machine heuristic" effects (Hovland & Weiss, 1951; Pornpitakpan, 2004; Sundar & Kim, 2019). People sometimes prefer algorithmic to human judgment ("algorithm appreciation"; Logg et al., 2019), although "algorithm aversion," harsher judgment of algorithms' mistakes than of humans', is also well documented (Dietvorst et al., 2015). Because participants in the original paradigm were told they would be talking to a neutral AI system (Costello et al., 2024), belief reductions may reflect deference to an apparently impartial, epistemically privileged source rather than (or in concert with) the content of the arguments (Glickman & Sharot, 2024).

A third class of accounts is interaction-based: the treatment's persuasive force depends on the dialogic, human-AI "chatbot" format. Conversations are a potent means of arbitrating between truth and falsehood (Mercier, 2016), and human-AI conversations are unusually personalized, judgment-free, socially warm, and non-adversarial, which may reduce defensiveness, increase engagement, or make participants feel understood (Sherman & Cohen, 2006). Sustained, respectful dialogue has also been argued to be central to the unusually durable attitude change produced by deep canvassing (Kalla & Broockman, 2020) and motivational interviewing (Miller & Rollnick, 2013). Dialogic AI's rhetorical fluency, long and informationally dense responses, and repeated back-and-forth opportunities to wear down objections could also drive the effect.

A final possibility is deflationary: the effect may partly reflect how belief was measured. In the original paradigm, participants named a conspiracy they believed and why, elaborated on their evidence, and only then rated their belief in an AI-generated restatement of their account. Because elaborating on a belief can itself prompt reflection or expose gaps in understanding (Fox et al., 2011; Sloman & Vives, 2022), the baseline rating, and the change measured from it, may depend on when elaboration occurs. And because the rated statement often incorporates the evidence participants cited, apparent change could reflect a narrow concession that those particular reasons were wrong, even if participants still hold the conspiracy to be true.

Current Research

To address these questions, we systematically manipulated core elements that relate to each underlying possible causal factor (or set of factors). Study 1 begins with a baseline treatment that closely replicates the original AI dialogue treatment from Costello et al. (2024), then varies its features one at a time (Table 1). Some conditions target the content of the AI's response: No Facts, in which the model is instructed to persuade without using rational argumentation or evidence; Just-the-Facts, in which the model is prompted to inform rather than persuade; and Concise, in which the model produces substantially shorter responses. Other conditions hold that content constant while varying how the AI or the interaction is framed to participants: Overt, in which participants are told of the AI's persuasive goal, and Adversarial, in which the exchange is framed as a debate. Two additional variations change the order of the conspiracy measurement and the specificity of the AI-generated belief summary. Each content or framing condition is therefore a targeted ablation: attenuated belief change relative to the Baseline treatment implicates the removed ingredient, whereas an unchanged effect shows that it was not necessary. Study 2 compares the Baseline treatment with (i) a single AI-generated message, to probe the importance of the dialogue, and (ii) a Control dialogue about an irrelevant topic, to rule out regression to the mean. We also analyze moderators (Study 2) and the content of the AI's responses (both studies) post hoc. & S2) A live, multi-turn dialogue in which the AI counters the participant's belief with reasons and evidence, replicating what led them to find that theory compelling, including any evidence, events, sources of information, or personal experiences they saw as supportive. Those responses were then passed to a live GPT-4-family model, which restated the participant's focal belief as a standardized psychometric item. Participants rated their confidence in that statement on a 0-100 scale (0 = Definitely false,50 = Uncertain,100 = Definitely true). This served as the pre-treatment belief measure. (In the Belief-First Rating condition of Study 1, the statement was generated from the first response alone and rated before the elaboration prompt.) To ensure that the paradigm targeted genuine conspiracy beliefs, participants' open-ended responses were also classified using the large language model (LLM)-assisted conspiracy-classification procedure described in Costello et al. (2024), which has shown strong convergence with human judgment (Gwet's AC1 = .80 [.72, .89]); Study 2 used gpt-4o with a more inclusive classification prompt (Supplemental Materials [SI] S1.3.2) and an altered restatement prompt. Articulating a conspiracy belief and rating the focal statement at or above the scale midpoint were preregistered analytic eligibility criteria applied to these pretreatment measures. Participants who failed attention checks or the writing-quality screener (both also preregistered), submitted duplicate responses, or used mobile devices were also excluded (SI S2.1). To prevent and detect automated or AI-generated responses, both studies began with a CAPTCHA, and the writing-quality screener was scored by an LLM instructed to flag incoherent, low-effort, or apparently AI-written answers (SI S1.3.3).

Immediately before the treatment, participants saw condition-specific instructions. Verbatim system-prompt and participant-facing instructions for every condition are reproduced in Supplemental Table S1; pre/post measurement items and attention checks are listed in SI S1.3, and key pretreatment moderator items are quoted under Measures below. In all active conditions, AI responses were generated live via API call to GPT-4 (Study 1) or GPT-5.2 (Study 2) rather than selected from a fixed bank of prewritten messages. Study 2 also included a Control condition wherein participants completed an AI conversation on a topic unrelated to conspiracy beliefs. To reduce the chance that results would depend on any one arbitrary control topic, control participants were randomly assigned to one of three unrelated conversation topics (experiences with the American medical system, views about firefighters, or cats versus dogs as companions). After the conversation with the AI, participants again rated belief in the focal conspiracy statement, providing the primary outcome measure. They then completed posttreatment questions; in Study 1, these included a brief open-ended explanation of why they did or did not find the AI's comments persuasive.

Study 1 was recruited from CloudResearch in two waves, each advertised as a 17.5minute study and run under one protocol, apart from two sentences of the No Facts prompt (SI S6.2; raw N = 3,988; 2,585 in Sample 1a and 1,403 in Sample 1b). The first wave (Sample 1a) was the original preregistered launch. Our preregistered allocation in Sample 1a was n = 200 per active condition and n = 400 in the Baseline treatment (more in the Baseline treatment to increase between-group precision). The preregistration listed ten conditions; two open-access model-comparison arms (Llama 3 70B and Llama 2 13B), together with an exploratory GPT-3.5 arm, were run only as a Sample 1a pilot (n = 173 after screening) and are excluded from all analyses (Figure S1). After Sample 1a closed, the rate at which open-ended responses were classified as a conspiracy belief was lower than our preregistration assumed, so we ran a second wave (Sample 1b). Sample 1b used weighted randomization that, after pooling across waves, brought each of the eight remaining conditions close to its target sample size; sample size was indexed to a power calculation on Sample 1a effect sizes, which found that n = 150 per active arm and n = 350 in the Baseline treatment gave 80% power to detect a halving of treatment efficacy relative to the Baseline treatment. The exclusions, applied in order, were: failed CAPTCHA (n = 8), accessed the survey on a mobile device (n = 20), did not reach or failed the embedded attention check (n = 297), did not reach the AI treatment (n = 163), assigned to the pilot model-comparison arms (n = 173), did not articulate a conspiracy belief (n = 1,828), pretreatment belief below 50/100 (n = 96), missing post-treatment belief (attrition; n = 38), and cross-wave duplicates dropped at the participant ID level (n = 75). The full cascade is shown in Figure S1. The final analytic sample was N = 1,290 (Sample 1a: 806; Sample 1b: 484). Percondition ns after pooling were Baseline treatment = 333, Abstracted Summary = 164, Belief-First Rating = 137, Concise = 137, Adversarial = 131, No Facts = 129, Overt = 132, Just-the-Facts = 127.

Study 2 was recruited from CloudResearch in a single wave under the same intake protocol as Study 1 (raw N = 2,082); the preregistered target was 400 conspiracy believers in each treatment arm and 200 in Control (the sensitivity of the achieved sample is reported with the Study 2 results). The exclusions, applied in order, were: mobile use at outset (n = 483); did not reach condition assignment (n = 136); not classified as a conspiracy belief by the LLM classifier (n = 296); pre-treatment belief below 50 (n = 126); failed at least one of two attention checks (n = 24); flagged the AI-generated summary of their belief as inaccurate (n = 88); and missing post-treatment belief (attrition; n = 26). The final analytic sample was N = 903. Percondition ns were Single Message = 364, Baseline treatment = 336, and Control = 203 (Cats / Dogs = 58; Firefighters = 75; Medical system = 70). The full cascade is shown in Figure S2.

Participants were U.S. adults recruited through CloudResearch Connect who completed the study in English; all descriptive statistics refer to the final analytic samples. In Study 1 (N = 1,290), mean age was 36.4 years (SD = 10.9); 54.9% identified as female, 43.4% as male, and 1.7% as nonbinary or another gender (or preferred not to say); 54.7% held a bachelor's degree or higher; 39.3% identified as Democrats, 29.4% as Independents, and 23.4% as Republicans; and 25.2% were Protestant and 13.6% Roman Catholic. Study 2 (N = 903) was sampled to balance partisanship (47.0% Democrat, 46.5% Republican); mean age was 40.9 years (SD = 13.8), 56.1% identified as female, 41.9% as male, and 2.0% as nonbinary or another gender (or preferred not to say), 57.0% held a bachelor's degree or higher, and 32.0% were Protestant and 18.7% Roman Catholic. Full demographic breakdowns by study (Table S4 and Figure S3) and a comparison of retained and excluded participants (Table S5) are reported in Supplemental Section S3.

Because demographic and AI-attitude items preceded the conspiracy elicitation, we could compare retained participants with those excluded for not articulating a conspiracy belief (tests uncorrected; Table S5). In Study 1, conspiracy-believing participants were more Republicanleaning (party preference d = 0.35; 39% vs. 52% Democrat), more religious (d = 0.19), and less often college graduates (55% vs. 61%; all ps < .001); in Study 2, which balanced party, differences were smaller (all |d| ≤ 0.14, all ps > .03; e.g., 56% vs. 63% female, p = .043). Age and trust in generative AI did not differ significantly between believers and non-believers in either study (|d| ≤ 0.12, all ps > .06), and no characteristic of either analytic sample shifted by more than 0.18 SD relative to all participants who provided demographics.

Measures

Focal belief (both studies). Participants rated the AI-generated summary of their belief: "On a scale of 0% to 100%, please indicate your level of confidence that this statement is true" (slider labeled Definitely false [0], Probably false, Uncertain [50], Probably true, Definitely true [100]). The item was repeated immediately after the AI interaction and serves as the primary outcome. Alongside the first rating, participants answered "Did the AI accurately summarize your perspective?" (yes/no).

Belief importance (both studies).

Participants answered "How important is this theory to your personal beliefs or understanding of the world?" (0 = Not at all important to my beliefs and worldview,2 = Slightly important,4 = Moderately important,6 = Very important,8 = Extremely important to my beliefs and worldview; odd values unlabeled). The item was asked before the AI interaction in both studies.

Trust in generative AI (both studies).

Before describing their belief, participants rated the single item "I generally trust new AI technologies like ChatGPT" (1 = Strongly disagree,2 = Disagree,3 = Somewhat disagree,4 = Neither agree nor disagree,5 = Somewhat agree,6 = Agree,7 = Strongly agree).

Study 2 moderators. After the first belief rating and before random assignment, participants answered "Have people close to you (e.g., friends/family) ever told you that they agree with this conspiracy theory?" (social embeddedness; a single 0-100 slider labeled No, none of them; Yes, 1-2 of them; Yes, all of them) and rated four statements on a 5-point scale (Strongly disagree, Somewhat disagree, Neither agree nor disagree, Somewhat agree, Strongly agree). "I have heard many arguments against this belief before" and "I have discussed this belief with people who disagreed with me" were averaged as prior exposure to counterarguments (r = .45, α = .62). "I have seriously considered whether this belief might be wrong" and "The arguments I've heard against this belief were weak or unconvincing" were analyzed as single items (SI S5.4a-S5.4b).

Participants' explanations (Study 1 only).

On the page after the post-treatment rating, participants whose rating had decreased were asked, "In just a sentence or two, would you mind explaining what about the AI's comments you found persuasive?" ; those whose rating was unchanged or higher were asked, "In just a sentence or two, would you mind explaining why you did not find the AI's comments to be persuasive?" Moderators from Costello et al. (2024). Two moderator analyses draw on data from the two experiments reported in Costello et al. (2024), which used the same paradigm. Participants in both of those experiments answered the same pretreatment trust-in-generative-AI item as in Study 2, which allows the trust analysis to pool three experiments. Participants in the first of those experiments were also recontacted roughly 12 days after treatment and completed the sixitem Actively Open-minded Thinking subscale of the Four-Component Thinking Styles Questionnaire (Newton et al., 2024;1 = Strongly disagree,2 = Disagree,3 = Somewhat disagree,4 = Somewhat agree,5 = Agree,6 = Strongly agree; all items reverse-scored; α = .91; highestloading item: "Regardless of the topic, what you believe to be true is more important than evidence against your beliefs").

Content Coding

Three post hoc content-coding schemes were applied by large language models at temperature 0 (SI S6.1, S7.1-S7.2, S9.1). First, to characterize the AI's persuasive approach, each conversation was rated on 16 persuasion strategies (e.g., presenting conflicting evidence, offering alternative explanations, building rapport, telling stories, citing credible sources). The list comes from the analysis code of Costello et al. (2024), where candidate strategies had been generated by repeatedly asking Turbo what strategies it would use to dissuade someone from a conspiracy belief and retaining the recurring ones. GPT-4o (gpt-4o-2024-08-06) rated each of the AI's three messages in each conversation on each strategy (none, low, moderate, high), one call per strategy per conversation; we analyze the ratings of the first message. Second, to characterize the content of the AI's messages at a finer grain, GPT-4.1 first extracted each specific factual claim in each AI message (excluding opinions, advice, and general rhetoric). Each distinct claim was then classified into one of six categories (specific empirical, inferentiallogical, meta-epistemic, moral/political, minimal/non-adjudicative, or general/underspecified) by Gemini 3 Flash. The categories were developed inductively. Third, to code participants' own explanations of why the AI was or was not persuasive, we developed 15 attributes (six positive, e.g., facts and logic, tailoring to the participant's claims; nine negative, e.g., perceived bias, lack of depth) by combining human review of the responses with candidate categories generated by an AI model given the same responses into a single taxonomy. GPT-4o judged each attribute present or absent in each response. The codebooks appear in Supplemental Tables S19, S23, and S31.

Results
