Preregistrations are available at https://aspredicted.org/hsrq-r9pc.pdf (Study 1) and https://aspredicted.org/8zz8rc.pdf (Study 2). All analyses other than the preregistered regressions (SI Tables S6 and S8) are post hoc.

Study 1 (N = 1,290) asks which features of the original dialogue are necessary by removing them one at a time, comparing each variant with the Baseline treatment. Study 2 (N = 903) asks whether dialogue itself is necessary (Single Message) and benchmarks the Baseline treatment against a dialogue on an unrelated topic (Control). We report each study's randomized contrasts in turn, then moderators, the content of the AI's responses, and participants' own accounts. Standardized within-condition pre-post reductions in Study 1 are reported as d av , the mean reduction divided by the average of the pre-and post-treatment SDs (Lakens, 2013; 95% CIs from a 5,000-resample percentile bootstrap), because post-treatment ratings were more variable than pre-treatment ratings, which the eligibility criterion confined to the upper half of the scale (e.g., Study 1 Baseline treatment, SD = 24.6 vs. 15.6). Contrasts between Study 2 conditions are reported as d ppc , the difference in mean pre-post reduction divided by the pooled pre-treatment SD. CrI is a credible interval; BF10 and BF01 are Bayes factors for and against a difference (SI S4.1); HC3 denotes heteroskedasticity-consistent standard errors.

Which Ingredients of the Treatment Were Necessary?

Participants in Study 1 typically entered the experiment with strong endorsement of their focal conspiracy theories (pretreatment M = 81.8 on the 0-100 belief scale). In the Baseline treatment, belief decreased from 80.5 to 69.6 following treatment, a mean reduction of 10.9 points (95% CI [8.65, 13.11], p < .001, d av = 0.54, 95% CI [0.45, 0.64]; Figure 1A). As in Costello et al. (2024), the reduction generalized across the specific conspiracy theories participants named. Its size varied by topic among treated participants (χ²(14, N = 1,807) = 63.1, p < .001), but in Study 2, which included a randomized control arm, the Baseline treatment effect did not differ significantly from control across topics (p = .274; see SI Sections S8.2-S8.3, Table S29 and Figure S9 for a per-conspiracy breakdown). The reduction also held at every level of pre-treatment belief, including the highest-conviction quintile (Figure 2). Among participants who rated their conspiracy as "very" to "extremely" important to their personal beliefs or understanding of the world (6-8 on the 0-8 importance item), the mean reduction across the seven evidence-based conditions (all but No Facts) was 8.03 points (95% CI [5.77, 10.30], p < .001, d av = 0.45, 95% CI [0.35, 0.55], n = 320; post hoc). In Study 2, the Baseline treatment effect did not vary significantly with belief importance (9.8 vs. 14.7 points relative to Control among participants above and below this "importance" threshold; Baseline treatment × Importance b = -2.43 per SD, 95% CI [-5.42, 0.57], p = .112, BF10 = 0.24) or with pre-treatment belief (Baseline treatment × Pre-treatment Belief p = .559, BF10 = 0.10; both post hoc).

To help contextualize the magnitude of these effects, we computed (post hoc) how many participants moved from one side of the scale midpoint to the other. Because participants could enter the study at the midpoint itself (50 = "Uncertain"; about 4% of each sample), we restrict this statistic to those whose pre-treatment rating was above 50, for whom a post-treatment rating below 50 indicates movement from the "true" side of the scale to the "false" side. In the Study 1 Baseline treatment, 16.5% of these participants (52 of 315) ended below the midpoint (percondition rates, and rates including participants who began at 50, in Supplemental Table S9). Note. Posterior distributions of belief reduction (pre -post, 0-100 scale) in each condition, from a Bayesian linear model controlling for pretreatment belief, fit separately for each study. Ridges are posterior densities; points mark posterior means (diamonds, the Baseline treatment; square, Control), and thick and thin bars show 80% and 95% credible intervals. Numbers at right give the posterior mean contrast with its 95% CrI against the Baseline treatment; positive values indicate a larger reduction than in the Baseline treatment. Note. Each panel shows one condition, plotting belief reduction (pre -post, 0-100 scale) against pretreatment belief. (A) Study 1 (N = 726): Baseline treatment, Just-the-Facts, Concise, and No Facts conditions. (B) Study 2 (N = 903): Baseline treatment, Single Message, and Control conditions. Solid lines and shaded bands are linear (ordinary least squares) fits of belief reduction on pretreatment belief within each condition, with 95% confidence bands. Points are mean belief reductions within quintiles of pretreatment belief (quintiles defined within study), plotted at the mean pretreatment belief of the participants in that cell; vertical bars are 95% confidence intervals. The dashed line repeats the Baseline treatment fit of the same study in the other panels for comparison. The fits and quintile means are descriptive (not model-adjusted), and the binning is post hoc.

Five Study 1 conditions were designed as targeted ablations of the Baseline treatment, and two altered the measurement procedure (Table 1). We compared the post-treatment belief score of each condition against the Baseline treatment while controlling for pre-treatment belief, and we report contrasts as differences in belief reduction, so that positive values indicate a larger reduction than in the Baseline treatment. In addition to the preregistered linear regressions, we also conducted complementary post hoc Bayesian regressions (Figure 1A). Given that many of the results are nulls, in the main text we focus on the Bayesian analyses as they quantify evidence for the null-difference interpretation rather than merely failure to reject it (full frequentist statistics for all contrasts are provided in Supplemental Tables S6 and S8, and are qualitatively equivalent). These contrasts could detect only large differences: each null contrast had 80% power for a difference of about 6-7 points, that is, 53-63% of the Baseline effect.

We observe a sharp asymmetry between the No Facts condition -in which the AI was instructed to persuade without relying on evidence or reasoned counterargument -and all other conditions. None of the conditions other than No Facts differed credibly from the Baseline treatment. Stripping the AI of explicit persuasive intent but still allowing it to provide topicrelevant facts and counterarguments (Just-the-Facts) did not detectably weaken the effect relative to the Baseline treatment (posterior mean difference = 0.55, 95% CrI [-3.73, 4.88], BF01 = 9.12), with a raw mean reduction of 11.62 points (95% CI [7.92, 15.32], p < .001, d av = 0.57, 95% CI [0.41, 0.74]). Instructing the AI to keep its responses "as short as possible while still being persuasive" (Concise), which roughly halved their length (M = 588 vs. 1,226 words per conversation) and reduced the number of extracted claims by about a third (17.7 vs. 26.2 per conversation; see the claim-level analysis below), did not detectably weaken the effect (posterior mean difference = 2.69, 95% CrI [-1.49, 6.90], BF01 = 4.37; raw mean reduction 13.83 points, 95% CI [9.57, 18.09], p < .001, d av = 0.65, 95% CI [0.49, 0.83]). Framing the exchange as a debate (Adversarial) did not detectably weaken the effect (posterior mean difference = 0.65, 95% CrI [-3.62, 4.86], BF01 = 8.75; raw reduction 11.85 points, 95% CI [8.02, 15.69], p < .001, d av = 0.59, 95% CI [0.43, 0.77]). Explicitly telling participants the AI would try to persuade them (Overt) did not detectably weaken the effect (posterior mean difference = -1.79, 95% CrI [-6.11, 2.48], BF01 = 6.52; raw reduction 9.10 points, 95% CI [5.74, 12.45], p < .001, d av = 0.46, 95% CI [0.32, 0.61]). The two measurement-procedure checks also matched the Baseline treatment. Instructing the summarizing model to omit participant-specific evidence from the rated conspiracy statement (Abstracted Summary) left the effect unchanged (posterior mean difference = 2.25, 95% CrI [-1.60, 6.12], BF01 = 5.28; raw reduction 13.31 points, 95% CI [9.72, 16.90], p < .001, d av = 0.66, 95% CI [0.52, 0.82]). Collecting the belief rating before the elaboration prompt (Belief-First Rating) did not alter the reduction (posterior mean difference = -0.79, 95% CrI [-4.86, 3.36], BF01 = 8.83; raw reduction 10.28 points, 95% CI [6.80, 13.75], p < .001, d av = 0.52, 95% CI [0.36, 0.68]); further, pre-treatment ratings in this condition did not differ significantly from the Baseline treatment (82.5 vs. 80.5; difference = 2.03, 95% CI [-0.95, 5.01], p = .18), and participants were no more likely to flag the shorter statement as an inaccurate summary of their belief (3.6% vs. 5.6% in the six conditions that used the standard procedure; Fisher's exact test, p = .422).

Conversely, the No Facts condition was much less effective than the Baseline treatment, with a posterior mean difference from the Baseline treatment of -7.17, 95% CrI [-11.39, -2.83] and strong evidence for a genuine difference (BF10 = 11.43). Raw mean reduction in No Facts was only 3.81 points (95% CI [1.36, 6.27]; one-sample t(128) = 3.08, p = .003; d av = 0.22, 95% CI [0.09, 0.35]), and only 5.7% of participants who began above the midpoint ended below it (vs. 16.5% in the Baseline treatment, Fisher's exact p = .003). Taken together, these results support evidence-based argumentation as a key driver of the AI's effect on conspiracy belief, while providing evidence against each of the other candidate explanations being necessary for a large effect. Specifically, the posterior probability of retaining at least half the Baseline effect was ≥ .97 for every variant other than No Facts (post hoc; Table S7). Attrition in Study 1 was 2.86% (38/1,328) and did not differ significantly across conditions, χ²(7, N = 1,328) = 4.05, p = .774 (per-condition rates in Supplemental Table S2), so differential attrition is not a concern in Study 1.

Participants in Study 2 likewise entered with strong endorsement of their focal conspiracy theories (pretreatment M = 80.7). In the Baseline treatment, belief decreased from 80.5 to 65.9, a mean reduction of 14.6 points, replicating Study 1, whereas the Control condition showed only a 1.4-point mean reduction (posterior mean difference from the Baseline treatment = -13.11, 95% CrI [-16.52, -9.71], BF10 > 1,000; Baseline treatment vs. Control d ppc = 0.88, 95% CI [0.69, 1.07], p < .001; Figure 1B); the Baseline effect is therefore not attributable to regression to the mean. Among Baseline-treatment participants who began above the scale midpoint, 23.1% (75 of 324) ended below it, compared with 1.5% (3 of 197) in Control (Fisher's exact p < .001; Supplemental Table S9). Study 2 then asked whether the dialogic format itself was critical. As in Study 1, each condition was compared with the Baseline treatment, controlling for pretreatment belief, with the Baseline treatment as the reference group (Figure 1B; Table S8). The Single Message produced a large reduction relative to Control (posterior mean difference = 10.28, 95% CrI [6.93, 13.65], BF10 > 1,000; Single Message vs. Control d ppc = 0.69, 95% CI [0.53, 0.87], p < .001), with a raw decrease of 11.76 points and 16.8% of participants who began above the midpoint ending below it. Compared with the Baseline treatment, the Single Message reduced belief by slightly less (posterior mean difference = -2.82, 95% CrI [-5.71, 0.06], BF01 = 2.1; HC3 b = 2.87 on the post-treatment belief scale, 95% CI [-0.36, 6.10], p = .081; d ppc = -0.19, 95% CI [-0.41, 0.02]), retaining an estimated 79% of the Baseline effect (95% CrI [58%, 101%]); this contrast had 80% power to detect a difference of about 4.6 points (35% of the Baseline effect). Thus, even when the treatment was reduced to a single tailored AI responsewith no iterative give-and-take, no ongoing rapport-building, and no dynamic responding -it produced a large reduction in conspiracy belief. Multi-turn dialogue is therefore not necessary to obtain a large effect, although it may confer some additional benefit beyond the static message.

Attrition in Study 2 differed by condition, χ²(2, N = 929) = 12.22, p = .002: Baseline treatment 5.08% (18/354), Single Message 0.82% (3/367), and Control 2.40% (5/208). To assess sensitivity to this differential attrition, we followed Costello et al. (2024) and conducted post hoc baseline-carried-forward-imputation analyses in which the 26 participants who dropped out after beginning the chat were assigned a post-treatment belief equal to their pre-treatment belief (i.e., a treatment effect of zero) and added to the analytic sample (N = 929). Treatment effects remained highly robust under this conservative assumption (post-treatment belief scale): Baseline treatment versus Control b = -12.51, p < .001; Single Message versus Control b = -10.29, p < .001 (full model with HC3 SEs in Supplemental Table S3). Differential attrition is therefore unlikely to be driving the Study 2 findings.

Moderators and Boundary Conditions (Study 2 and External Data)

Study 2 also allowed us to test, post hoc, several boundary-condition accounts of the treatment (Figure 3). Each moderator below is reported both as a frequentist test with HC3 standard errors (b, 95% CI, p) and a Bayes factor (BF10 ) from a Bayesian model (priors in SI S4.1; SI Tables S18a and S18b).

A first possibility is that the effect relies on people deferring to AI as a trustworthy source. Because Study 2 and both experiments of Costello et al. (2024) measured trust in generative AI with the same pretreatment item, we pooled the three experiments for a higherpowered test (post hoc; n = 2,594). Each experiment included a treatment arm and randomly assigned control arm, with the model including experiment fixed effects and experiment-specific treatment effects and trust slopes (i.e., treatment-control contrasts are made within an experiment; arm sizes and specification in SI S5.1, Table S11). We found a positive interaction between trust in AI and treatment (posterior mean = 2.13 points per trust scale point, 95% CrI [1.09, 3.16], BF10 > 30; HC3 b = 2.13, 95% CI [1.29, 2.98], p < .001; Figure 3A), which did not differ detectably across the three experiments (p = .317). Substantively, however, the treatment remained highly effective even among participants with low trust in AI (e.g., 10.7 belief points at 1 SD below mean AI trust, 95% CrI [8.3, 13.1]). Greater trust in AI may modestly facilitate the treatment but is not necessary for it.

A second possibility is that the social context in which a belief is held bounds the treatment's efficacy, such that beliefs reinforced by one's social network do not respond to correction. Social embeddedness of the focal belief did not linearly predict a smaller Baseline treatment effect: the Treatment × Social Embeddedness interaction was not credibly different from zero (posterior mean = -0.065 points of belief reduction per 1-point increase in embeddedness, 95% CrI [-0.188, 0.060]; BF10 = 0.15 for the interaction expressed per SD of embeddedness; HC3 b = -1.81 per SD, 95% CI [-4.84, 1.21], p = .240; Figure 3B). Even in the top quartile of embeddedness (≥ 80 on the 0-100 scale; n = 229), the Baseline treatment produced a substantial reduction relative to Control (b = -7.30 on the post-treatment belief scale, p = .001). Relatedly, a two-item composite measuring prior exposure to counterarguments did not moderate the treatment effect (posterior mean of the Treatment × Prior-Exposure interaction = -0.63 points of belief reduction per scale point, 95% CrI [-3.56, 2.27], BF10 = 0.08; HC3 b = -0.71 per SD, 95% CI [-3.47, 2.06], p = .617; SI S5.4, Table S15, Figure S5).

The account developed so far -that the treatment works by supplying diagnostic counterevidence to people open to revising their beliefs -makes one further prediction: the effect should be larger for individuals more inclined to engage in deliberation in order to form more accurate beliefs (Pennycook, 2023). We could examine this only indirectly, using data from Study 1 of Costello et al. (2024) not previously reported for this question: participants in that study were recontacted roughly 12 days after the experiment and completed the Actively Open-minded Thinking (AOT) subscale of the Four-Component Thinking Styles Questionnaire (Newton et al., 2024; see Measures). Regressing immediate post-treatment belief change on condition, AOT, their interaction, and pretreatment belief in a Bayesian model with weakly informative priors (treatment n = 365, control n = 258), we found that a 1-SD increase in AOT was associated with a 3.72-point larger treatment effect (posterior mean; 95% CrI [0.54, 6.95], BF10 = 1.09; HC3 b = 3.73, 95% CI [0.96, 6.51], p = .009; Figure 3C; SI S5.3), although the effect remained sizable at 1 SD below the mean (12.2 points). Because AOT was measured after the conversation, the direction of this association is uncertain -participants who had been persuaded may afterwards have described themselves as more open-minded -although AOT did not differ by condition (d = 0.04, 95% CI [-0.12, 0.20], p = .605) and follow-up completion did not differ across arms (78.0% vs. 78.7%, p = .822), which makes that reading less likely.

In Study 2, we found no significant moderation of the Baseline treatment effect by pretreatment belief (b = -0.86 points per SD, 95% CI [-3.77, 2.04], p = .559), education (b = -2.60, 95% CI [-5.27, 0.07], p = .056), religiosity (b = -0.94, 95% CI [-3.92, 2.04], p = .537), age (b = -0.39, 95% CI [-3.25, 2.47], p = .790), or party identification (Republicans vs. Democrats: b = -4.52, 95% CI [-10.22, 1.18], p = .120), all with BF10 ≤ 0.30 per SD, and the effect did not differ significantly across conspiracy topics (p = .274; few participants per topic, SI S5.5). Women showed a larger effect than men (b = 8.34, 95% CI [2.76, 13.93], p = .003, BF10 = 1.72 per SD), yet this difference did not appear in Study 1 (association p = .909) or in either experiment of Costello et al. (2024; interaction ps = .959 and .625; Table S18b). Participants who rated the counterarguments they had previously heard as weak or unconvincing showed a smaller effect (b = -3.51 per SD, 95% CI [-6.39, -0.64], p = .017, BF10 = 0.84).

What Was the AI Actually Saying? (Content Analyses)

To better understand how the AI reduced conspiracy beliefs -and why the No Facts condition in particular was much less effective -we conducted two complementary post hoc content analyses. We first characterize the dialogues at the level of broad persuasive strategies (Study 1), then drill down to the level of individual claims (Studies 1 and 2). The coding procedures are described in the Method.

Strategy-level analysis (Study 1).

We focused on the four Study 1 conditions that most directly manipulated the AI's conversational behavior (Baseline treatment, Just-the-Facts, No Facts, Concise), analyzed as randomized within the analytic sample (n = 309, 111, 116, and 123, respectively; N = 659). GPT-4o rated the AI's first message in each conversation on 16 candidate persuasion strategies (four-level ordinal scale: none / low / moderate / high; definitions of all 16 strategies in Supplemental Table S19). Because many strategies were conceptually overlapping, we summarized them using a polychoric-correlation principal component analysis (PCA) with varimax rotation. A parallel analysis indicated five components, which together accounted for 76% of the variance (Figure 4A). Based on the loadings, we labeled these Facts & Evidence (rebutting the participant's specific claims with contradicting evidence, alternative explanations, and logical flaws -that is, factual and reasoned rebuttal); Sources & Experts (citing credible sources, expert consensus, and further resources); Rapport / Common Ground (validating the participant, finding shared values, and attending to the feelings behind the belief); Stories / Metaphors / Examples (anecdotes, analogies, and illustrations); and Harms & Empathy (asking the participant to consider how the belief affects other people, and describing the harms of conspiracy beliefs).

Strategy use differed across the four conditions on every component (all omnibus ps < .001; Figure 4B; all 30 pairwise contrasts in SI Section S6.2), in ways that closely tracked the prompt manipulations. Relative to the Baseline treatment, No Facts was 1.90 SDs lower on Facts & Evidence and 0.77 SDs lower on Sources & Experts -the lowest of the four conditions on both -and 1.83 SDs higher on Stories / Metaphors / Examples and 0.58 SDs higher on Harms & Empathy (all Holm-adjusted ps < .001); the two conditions did not differ on Rapport / Common Ground (-0.03 SDs, Holm-adjusted p = .533). When told not to rely on evidence, the model stated its disagreement, as its prompt instructed, and then turned to analogies and stories about false beliefs and to appeals to feeling. Namely, it acknowledged the emotions behind the belief and asked participants to think about the people affected by the events in question (91% of No Facts first messages; e.g., the victims and first responders of 9/11) and, less often, about how the theory itself might weigh on the believer or hurt those people (19% of No Facts first messages). No Facts almost never cited sources or expert consensus (representative excerpts from No Facts conversations are reproduced in Supplemental Table S33).

Just-the-Facts moved in an orthogonal direction: it did not differ significantly from the Baseline treatment on Facts & Evidence (0.17 SDs lower, Holm-adjusted p = .140) but was 1.42 SDs lower on Rapport / Common Ground (Holm-adjusted p < .001). Concise behaved like Just-the-Facts: relative to the Baseline treatment it was only 0.35 SDs lower on Facts & Evidence but 1.12 SDs lower on Rapport / Common Ground (and 0.31 and 0.35 SDs lower on Sources & Experts and Harms & Empathy; all Holmadjusted ps ≤ .015), and it did not differ from Just-the-Facts on any component (all Holmadjusted ps ≥ .092). That is, when told to be brief, the model kept its evidence-based content and cut social and rhetorical material.

We next asked which of these strategy families were associated with larger belief changes. We fit a five-mediator path model (ML estimation) in which the five PCA strategy components served as parallel mediators between the prompt manipulation and belief change, with mediator residual covariances freely estimated (SI S6.4, Table S21). The model simultaneously estimates (i) the effect of each condition on each strategy component (five apaths per condition), (ii) the partial association of each strategy component with belief change holding condition and the other four components constant (the five b-paths reported below), and (iii) the residual direct effect of each condition on belief change. Model specification was saturated, so we report parameters but not model fit (standard errors and 95% CIs are computed from a 5,000-resample bootstrap). Across the five b-paths the pattern was asymmetric (Table S21). Two components were positively associated with belief reduction: Facts & Evidence (b = 4.85 per SD, 95% CI [2.29, 7.25], p < .001) and Sources & Experts (b = 3.24, 95% CI [1.39, 5.20], p = .001). These two coefficients did not differ significantly from each other (difference = 1.61, 95% CI [-1.37, 4.35], p = .269), but each exceeded each of the other three (differences 3.35-5.94, all unadjusted 95% CIs excluding zero; omnibus test of equal coefficients χ²(4, N = 658) = 14.41, p = .006; Table S22). The coefficients for Rapport / Common Ground (b = -0.11, 95% CI [-2.19, 1.86], p = .916), Stories / Metaphors / Examples (b = -1.09, 95% CI [-2.85, 0.78], p = .238), and Harms & Empathy (b = -0.83, 95% CI [-2.61, 0.92], p = .355) were near zero and did not differ among themselves (joint test p = .747).

Indeed, in that model's decomposition, No Facts' lower Facts & Evidence score carried the largest indirect association with belief change (b indirect = -11.07, 95% CI [-16.83, -5.17]; 69% of the summed indirect paths), with Sources & Experts accounting for most of the remainder. A complementary generalized additive model -which relaxes the linearity assumption and estimates each strategy's partial contribution non-parametrically -showed the same positive associations for Facts & Evidence and Sources & Experts (both ps < .001; Figure 5). It also showed a small negative association for Harms & Empathy (p = .001; SI S6.3). S20). Note. Each panel shows the estimated partial effect of one strategy component (its score in the AI's first message, in SD units) on belief reduction (pre -post, 0-100 scale), adjusting for pretreatment belief and the other four components but not for condition. Zero on the vertical axis marks the sample-average reduction, not the absence of change. Lines are truncated to the empirical range of each component, and the horizontal axis is cut at ±2.4 SD, which omits about 2%-4% of scores per component. Shaded ribbons are pointwise 95% confidence intervals; tick marks along the horizontal axis show the observed component scores. N = 1,174 rated conversations in the Study 1 analytic sample (all eight conditions; SI S6.3).

Claim-level analysis (Studies 1 and 2).

Strategy labels still leave open a substantive question: What did "facts and evidence" actually consist of? To answer this question, we passed each saved AI message to GPT-4.1 and had it extract the specific factual claims in that message (the extraction prompt excluded opinions, advice, and general rhetoric). We then deduplicated the claim texts and coded them with Gemini 3 Flash using a codebook with six top-level categories: specific empirical claims, inferential-logical claims, meta-epistemic claims, moral/political evaluation claims, minimal / non-adjudicative claims, and general or underspecified claims (see Table 2 for examples; the codebook is in SI Section S7.2, Table S23, with subtypes in S7.7).

Specific empirical

The Apollo flag had a horizontal rod along the top edge to hold it out.

According to the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), the steel's temperature reached up to 1,000°C.

Inferential / logical It would require keeping a secret amongst thousands of people for over 50 years if the moon landing were faked. If a claim is true, there should be a testable paper trail identifying the specific location, procedure, audit, or court filing with supporting records.

Meta-epistemic Verified evidence of alien life would require evidence that clearly cannot be explained by anything else and can be checked by many independent experts. General mistrust of billionaires or tech is not evidence; motive is not evidence and capability alone is not evidence.

Moral / political evaluation

Upholding the rule of law, ensuring judicial independence, and engaging in constructive, informed discourse are pathways to reinforcing democratic governance. If exposed, allowing such an attack would be one of the biggest crimes in US history.

Minimal / nonadjudicative

Federalism distributes power between national and state governments.

Parkland Hospital is the same hospital where Kennedy and later Oswald were taken.

General / underspecified

Governments do sometimes create confusing or misleading paperwork for ordinary reasons like protecting sources, covering embarrassing mistakes, or hiding human technology. Most evidence for aliens at Area 51 is anecdotal and hard to verify.

Note.

Each example is a verbatim AI claim. Examples are sampled at random within type from claims in the top quartile of the classifier's confidence ratings (SI S7.2).

The pipeline yielded 30,662 claim instances (30,314 distinct claim texts) in Study 1 and 27,535 claim instances (27,352 distinct claim texts) in Study 2. Pooling across all Study 1 conditions, the distribution of distinct claims was 47.4% specific empirical, 24.1% minimal / non-adjudicative, 15.2% general/underspecified, 6.2% moral/political, 4.1% inferential-logical, and 2.9% meta-epistemic. The share of specific empirical claims ranged from 44.5% in Concise to 53.4% in Just-the-Facts across most conditions (47.6% in the Baseline treatment; percondition composition in Table S24), but collapsed in No Facts -only 9.9% of No Facts claims were specific empirical, averaging just 0.7 such claims per conversation (vs. about 13 specificempirical claims per conversation across the other Study 1 conditions). Concise, by contrast, reduced the volume of content while largely preserving its profile: 52% fewer words and 32% fewer claims than the Baseline treatment (17.7 vs. 26.2 claims per conversation). Together with the experimental contrasts, this indicates that the full amount of information the Baseline AI provided is not required for a comparable effect.

Study 2, which used GPT-5.2 rather than GPT-4, showed a similar pattern. Across the treated Study 2 corpus, the mix was 37.2% specific empirical, 22.5% minimal / non-adjudicative, 18.9% general/underspecified, 16.4% inferential-logical, 3.3% meta-epistemic, and 1.7% moral/political.

Participants' Own Accounts of What Was Persuasive (Study 1)

As a final post hoc analysis, we examined the free-text responses collected at the end of Study 1 (Study 2 did not include these items). Participants whose post-treatment rating was lower than their pre-treatment rating were asked what about the AI's comments they found persuasive (n = 584 with coded responses), and those whose rating was unchanged or higher were asked why they found the comments unpersuasive (n = 474; see Measures). As described in the Method, GPT-4o coded each response for the presence or absence of each of 15 attributes covering positive themes (e.g., factual content, tailored argument, expertise, emotional synchrony) and criticisms (e.g., perceived bias, repetitiveness, off-topic drift, obsequiousness; full codebook in Table S31). Persuaded participants overwhelmingly cited the AI's provision of tailored facts, evidence, and logic, with far fewer mentioning its trustworthiness or expertise (Figure 6). Unpersuaded participants also cited informational factors such as lack of depth and insufficient evidence, while also bringing up perceived lack of trustworthiness and objectivity on the part of the AI. Codes were not mutually exclusive (SI Figure S10), and thus some responses included an attribute from the "opposite" column: 11.2% of unchanged-or-increased participants mentioned at least one positive attribute, and 15.1% of belief-reduced participants mentioned at least one criticism. Most of these are mixed appraisals (praise with a caveat, or a small reduction accompanied by a complaint). Criticisms appeared in 22% of participants whose belief fell by 20 points or less but in 6% of those who moved more (Fisher's exact p < .001). These analyses thus further emphasize the importance of evidence and reasoning for belief updating, while also being consistent with our observation that trust in AI moderates the treatment effect to some extent.

Figure 6

Attributes Participants Named When Explaining Why the AI Was or Was Not Persuasive (Study 1) Note. Bars show the percentage of participants in each column whose written explanation was coded as mentioning the attribute (GPT-4o, one call per attribute per response). Codes are not mutually exclusive so a response may contribute to several bars or to none (SI Figure S10 shows how often attributes were mentioned together). Participants are the Study 1 analytic sample excluding No Facts, restricted to the 1,058 with a non-empty explanation and cached codes (codes exist for 1,061 of 1,161 eligible participants; three blank responses were excluded). Top: positive attributes; bottom: negative attributes. Attribute labels are short names for the codes defined in Table S31 (e.g., "Perceived AI agreement": the participant perceived the AI as agreeing with them). Error bars are 95% Wilson intervals. Attributes appearing in the opposite column (e.g., a criticism from a participant whose belief fell) are retained as coded (SI S9.3).

Discussion

Previous work has established that conversations with generative AI can substantially reduce belief in conspiracies (Costello et al., 2024). Here we probed a set of theoretical and methodological claims about why this effect occurs. In Study 1, six of seven variationsremoving the AI's apparent neutrality, its non-adversarial framing, its persuasive intent, and roughly half of its verbosity, and altering two features of the measurement procedure -left the effect intact; only instructing the AI to persuade without evidence, counterargument, or reasoning sharply weakened it. In analyses of what the AI said, belief change tracked its reliance on evidence and reasoned rebuttal, not rapport-building, storytelling, or appeals to empathy, and persuaded participants most often cited its factual arguments. We thus find strong evidence that an evidence-rich argumentative package featuring facts is a necessary and central mechanism of the effect.

In Study 2, a single tailored message retained an estimated 79% of the full dialogue's effect and far exceeded an unrelated-topic control dialogue, in which belief did not move. Dialogue may confer some additional benefit, but much of the effect can be achieved with a single message tailored to the specific belief.

Our data also argue against several other candidate features being necessary for a large debunking effect: the AI's rhetorical abilities (arranging words persuasively with substantive content held fixed), its ability to converse, its rapport and social warmth, the machine heuristic or algorithm aversion, measurement error, regression to the mean, and information overload. That said, our studies could detect only large differences between treatment arms (about half to twothirds of the Baseline effect in Study 1 and about one third in Study 2), and we did not experimentally increase these elements or manipulate them in combination. Individual features, including multi-turn dialogue and trust in AI, may therefore contribute to the effect but to a smaller degree. Identifying the potentiating and inhibitory features of AI-based argumentation is a fruitful direction for future research (Jones & Bergen, 2024). Nonetheless, what we do show is that none of these elements are necessary for a large effect. Nor do they themselves evidently produce large effects.

Recent external findings converge with this picture. First, in the same paradigm, believing one was talking to a human expert rather than an AI produced comparable belief reductions (Boissin et al., 2025), so the effect does not rely on deference to AI as a source. Second, the effect persisted without substantive change at a 2-month follow-up (Costello et al., 2024), suggesting that the updating is not a fleeting demand artifact. Third, research in other domains has found support for the role of facts and evidence in AI-mediated belief updating. Lin et al. (2025) found that depriving AI canvassers of factual claims dramatically reduced their persuasiveness in the 2025 Canadian national and Polish presidential elections, and Hackenburg et al. (2025) found that instructing an AI to persuade with large quantities of facts and evidence was the most effective strategy on UK political issues. However, despite these few examples, future work is needed to map how central evidence-based argumentation is to belief change across contexts.

Our results suggest that many conspiracy believers are substantially more evidenceresponsive than the standard portrait of motivated reasoning, epistemic closure, and self-sealing worldviews implies (Biddlestone et al., 2021; Lewandowsky et al., 2013; Sunstein & Vermeule, 2009). Participants low in trust in AI, with highly socially embedded beliefs, or previously familiar with anti-conspiracy arguments showed large belief updates. Although the effect was directionally smaller for beliefs rated very to extremely important to one's worldview, it was still large (in Study 2, 9.8 vs. 14.7 points relative to Control for less important beliefs; interaction p = .112).

Data from Costello et al. (2024, Study 1) are also consistent with individual variation in the propensity to update: participants higher in actively open-minded thinking (the disposition to revise beliefs according to evidence; Pennycook et al., 2020; Stanovich & Toplak, 2019) showed larger belief revision, though the effect was substantial even at low levels of the trait. However, since the trait was measured after treatment, the direction of this association is uncertain and requires further research.

Our work has several limitations. First, participants self-selected into online studies and may not represent the most committed believers. Nonetheless, they enrolled without knowing that the studies concerned conspiracy beliefs, and relatively few dropped out once they learned this. Relatedly, the sample skews modestly more female (55% vs. 51% of U.S. adults; U.S. Census Bureau, 2024), substantially more educated (56% vs. 36% of U.S. adults aged 25 and older with a bachelor's degree or higher), and substantially less religious (45% vs. 62% Christian; Pew Research Center, 2025) than population estimates; if the treatment's effect varies with these characteristics, our effect sizes may not generalize without adjustment to a probability sample of U.S. adults. However, moderation tests showed no reliable and significant differences in effect by demographic characteristics, suggesting that the core finding generalizes at least across the demographic range we observed. Also, the laboratory isolates participants from social and informational networks that may reinforce such beliefs, although the Baseline treatment remained effective even for the most socially embedded beliefs, which addresses the strongest version of this concern.

Second, the No Facts prompt removes a bundle of co-occurring features -specific facts, source citations, claim-level precision -rather than any one of them cleanly, so "facts" here should be read as a set of correlated properties rather than a surgical ablation; and the claim-level analyses, being observational, carry a weaker causal warrant than the experimental contrasts. Self-reported prior exposure to counterarguments did not moderate the effect, and halving the length of the AI's responses did not detectably weaken it, consistent with the possibility that what matters is not the volume of facts per se but the right counterevidence. Future work could test this by measuring familiarity with the AI's arguments and by varying the amount of evidence more finely and its selection, specificity, sourcing, framing, and personalization factorially.

Even so, the overall picture is difficult to square with a strong-form motivated-reasoning account of conspiracy belief. Given relevant, specific, personalized counterevidence, many firm conspiracy believers update substantially: In Study 2, nearly a quarter (23%) of Baselinetreatment participants who began above the scale midpoint ended below it. Conspiracy beliefs do not appear to blind all believers to evidence; instead, conspiracies may survive in an epistemic environment where relevant counterevidence and argument, appropriately framed, are rarely delivered. Generative AI models are remarkably good at addressing this deficit.

Overview

Analyses were implemented in R (4.5) unless otherwise specified; code and data are described in §S11.

S1. Overview of the Experimental Procedure

S1. Overview of the Experimental Procedure

S1.1 Study 1: Flow and Participant Eligibility

Study 1 ran in two waves from the same participant pool under one protocol, apart from two sentences of the No Facts prompt (Samples 1a and 1b; §S6.2). After giving consent, participants completed a CAPTCHA; those who failed it or opened the survey on a mobile device were screened out ( §S2.1). They then answered an open-ended writing screener ( §S1.3.3), demographic and political items that included an attention check, and questions about their familiarity with, use of and trust in AI ( §S1.3.5). Participants were then randomly assigned to a condition (Table S1; a ninth, alternative-model arm in Sample 1a is excluded, §S2.1).

Next, participants described a conspiracy theory they found credible and elaborated on what led them to believe it (wording in §S1.3.4). GPT-4 (gpt-4-0125-preview, also used for the Study 1 classifier and dialogue) summarized the two answers in one sentence (prompts in §S1.3.1). Participants rated their confidence that this statement was true (0-100) and said whether the summary was accurate. In the Belief-First Rating condition the order differed: participants answered the initial conspiracy prompt, rated a statement that was generated from that first response alone (a one-question variant of the summarization prompt, reproduced in §S1.3.1), and only then completed the elaboration prompt, before the dialogue. The rated statement in Belief-First Rating was therefore shorter (22.2 vs. 32.2 words); participants flagged it as an inaccurate summary no more often than in the six standard-procedure arms (3.6% vs. 5.6%; Fisher's exact p = .42). In the Abstracted Summary condition, the summarization prompt also told the AI not to mention any specific evidence the participant had given ( §S1.3.1).

In Study 1, GPT-4 then judged whether the answers expressed a conspiracy belief, using the YES/NO prompt validated in Costello et al. (2024; §S1.3.2); participants judged not to were routed to parallel questions and a parallel dialogue about a non-conspiratorial belief.

Participants with a conspiracy belief then saw condition-specific instructions and had three rounds of chat with GPT-4, whose system prompt differed by condition (Table S1). After the chat they rated the same statement again (0-100). Those whose rating had fallen were asked what they found persuasive in the AI's comments, and the others why they did not ( §S1.3.4).

S1.2 Study 2: Flow and Participant Eligibility

Study 2 followed the Study 1 procedure, except as follows. On the same page as the pretreatment belief rating, before random assignment, participants also answered two measures used as moderators ( §S5): four items on prior exposure to counterarguments against their belief, and one item on whether close others (friends, family) endorse it (social embeddedness; wording in the main text). The restatement and classifier prompts were gpt-4o variants (classifier wording in §S1.3.2), a second attention check was added ( §S1.3.5), and there were no open-ended persuasion questions.

Participants were randomly assigned to one of three conditions, all run with GPT-5.2 (gpt-5.2-2025-12-11). The Baseline treatment (labeled Full Dialogue in some tables) was the same three-round conversation as in Study 1. In Single Message, participants read one personalized AI message tailored to the reasons they had given, with no further exchange. In Control, participants had a conversation of comparable format on one of three unrelated topics, assigned at random: the American medical system, firefighters, or cats versus dogs. Belief was measured again immediately after the AI interaction in every condition.

The purpose of this dialogue is to see how AI can engage around complicated topics. Please be open and honest in your responses, and remember that the AI is neutral and non-judgmental. Your participation is confidential. Thank you for contributing to this study on AI and human interaction." The AI then opened with: "Hello, welcome to the study! To start, will you provide me with a quick overview of your experiences with the medical system (doctors, hospitals, medication) in the United States?" Control -Firefighters Same as for the medical system, with "firefighters" in place of "the American medical system". The AI then opened with: "Hello, welcome to the study! To start, will you provide me with a quick overview of your thoughts or experiences with fire fighting professionals in the United States?" Control -Cats vs. Dogs Same as for the medical system, with "your views on pets -particularly, cats vs. dogs" in place of "your views and experiences with the American medical system", and "debate with human beings around various topics" in place of "engage around complicated topics". The AI then opened with: "Hello, welcome to the study! To start, will you tell me about whether you prefer dogs or cats? Why?"

Single Message

Baseline treatment prompt, with "Do not conclude with a follow-up question to the user." added at the end. The exchange ended after this one AI message. Control -American medical system "Your objective is to engage deeply with users about their personal experiences and perceptions of the American medical system. Facilitate a thoughtful and nuanced discussion that allows the user to thoroughly express and elaborate on their experiences, views, and the impact of these experiences on their beliefs about healthcare. Use simple language that an average person will be able to understand, ensuring the conversation is accessible yet profound. Encourage users to share detailed accounts of their interactions with the medical system, including positive aspects, challenges faced, and how these experiences have shaped their understanding and trust in healthcare services. Your questions should be open-ended, prompting reflection on personal stories, the role of healthcare professionals in their lives, and the accessibility and quality of medical care they have received. While maintaining a focus on healthcare experiences, carefully navigate the discussion to avoid leading it toward conspiracy theories, politics, religion, or any other sensitive subjects that are unrelated to their direct experiences with the American medical system. Aim to understand the underlying values, concerns, and hopes that inform the user's perspectives on healthcare, fostering a conversation that mirrors the engagement and depth of the treatment condition. This discussion should offer insights into the complexities of navigating the healthcare system, the emotional and practical implications of these experiences, and the diverse viewpoints that individuals hold based on their personal journeys through the medical landscape. Your goal is to create a space for users to reflect on and articulate their healthcare experiences in a way that highlights the human aspect of navigating the American medical system, encouraging a dialogue that is both informative and empathetic. Avoid special characters since your responses will be displayed in plain text." Control -Firefighters "Your objective is to engage deeply with users about their experiences and beliefs related to firefighters. While avoiding discussions leading toward conspiracy theories, politics, religion, or any sensitive subjects, craft your questions and responses to encourage detailed storytelling and reflection. Prompt the user to think critically about their experiences and the impact of firefighters on their life and community. Your goal is to create a rich conversation that allows individuals to explore and articulate their thoughts in a nuanced way. Avoid special characters since your responses will be displayed in plain text." Control -Cats vs. Dogs "Your objective is to engage in a thoughtful and stimulating debate with users about whether cats or dogs are better companions. You should carefully listen to the key points of the user's argument and perspective, encouraging them to elaborate on their reasoning and experiences that lead to their preference. Use simple language that an average person will be able to understand. Avoid special characters since your responses will be displayed in plain text."

S1.3 Verbatim Wording of AI-Pipeline Prompts and Measurement Items

Prompts and items are verbatim (em dashes replace double hyphens) and identical across studies unless noted. "[text of QID50]" is that question's wording and "[QID50 response]" the participant's answer. Items quoted in the main text are not repeated.

S1.3.1 Summarization Prompts

Study 1, standard prompt (all arms but the two below): "Summarize the following passage, which describes a conspiratorial belief, in a single sentence. Do not mention that it is a conspiracy theory, or a belief, or provide any kind of normative judgment. Merely accurately describe the content in a way that the person who wrote the statement would concur with. Frame it as an assertion. If the statement is already short, no need to change it very much. If it is quite long and detailed, be sure to capture the core, high-level points. Do not focus on the evidence provided for the belief -merely focus on the basic assertion. For your context, the passage was written by a participant in an online academic survey in response to the following two questions: < Q1: [text of QID50] > < Q2: [text of QID51] > Again, your role is to summarize the overarching belief captured by this response. Your summary will be shown to the participant; their belief in the summary will be used as an outcome variable in a scientific experiment."

Study 1, Belief-First Rating (first response only): as the standard prompt, but with one question: "in response to the following question: < Q1: [text of QID157] >" (QID157 repeats QID50).

Study 1, Abstracted Summary: as the standard prompt, with these changes:  It begins "Summarize the following text" and reads "the content of the statement" for "the content".  "Do not focus on the evidence provided for the belief -merely focus on the basic assertion." is replaced by "IMPORTANT: Do not mention any specific evidence provided for the belief unless it is integral to the overarching conspiracy theory; that is, you should only summarize the broader claim or point. ALSO IMPORTANT: do not mention the context of the statement in your summary (i.e., refrain from saying things like <the assertion is that> at the outset of your summary)."  The sentence beginning "Again, your role" ends "-DO NOT MENTION ANY SPECIFIC EVIDENCE."

S1.3.2 Conspiracy Classifier

S1.3.2 Conspiracy Classifier "Determine if the following question-responses (from a participant in an online academic survey) collectively contain or reflect AFFIRMATIVE belief in a conspiracy theory (or something quite like a conspiracy theory).

Study 1:

For context, the respondent has been asked the following two questions. < Q1: [text of QID50] > < Q2: [text of QID51] > The participant's answers to these questions are piped in as the first user response of this chat. If they contain or reflect affirmative belief in a conspiracy theory (or something quite like a conspiracy theory), respond only YES; else, respond only NO."

In about the first 20 hours of Sample 1a the in-survey classifier call failed in the Belief-First Rating and Abstracted Summary arms; those records were classified later, except 47 that never were (excluded).

Study 2:

"You are classifying responses from participants in an online survey about conspiracy theories. Participants answered two questions: (Q1) Asked participants explicitly to briefly describe a conspiracy theory they think is true (Q2) Asked participants to discuss specific reasons for their belief.

Your task is to determine whether the participant's responses indeed reflect belief in a conspiracy theory or a closely related idea.

Use the everyday (folk) concept of a conspiracy theory, not a strict academic definition. Borderline or loosely related scenarios, like cryptid sightings, paranormal phenomena, or secretive cover-ups, are acceptable and should be classified as TRUE. Classify as FALSE if: -The participant explicitly expresses disbelief or rejection of the conspiracy theory without any sign of uncertainty or ambivalence. -The participant says that they don't really hold conspiracy beliefs or seem to misread the question or similar Classify as INVALID if /n -The participant's responses are nonsensical, random, clearly inattentive, irrelevant, or obviously off-topic. /n -Responses appear auto-generated, robotic, spam-like, include characters or script a human is unlikely to use, or otherwise clearly indicate lack of genuine engagement.

If unclear, default to classifying responses as TRUE, favoring false positives. Only respond with TRUE or FALSE or INVALID"

S1.3.3 Writing-Screener Prompt

"Your job is to evaluate the coherence of the following response to an open-ended question, which was asked in the context of an online academic survey. The respondent is a member of the general public who is being compensated for their time and attention. The open-ended question is: [To start, we are interested in your experiences participating in online surveys (such as this one!). Why are you taking this survey? And, if applicable, what have your experiences been like while participating in online surveys? Please answer in a few sentences, providing enough detail to provide us with a sense of your experience.

] IN YOUR RESPONSE, AS A LLM, PROVIDE ONLY A NUMERIC RATING FOR THE COHERENCE OF THE PARTICIPANT'S RESPONSE.

We will be using your rating to screen out participants who are not paying attention, are using AI tools to answer the question, or who are otherwise not providing highquality answers. AGAIN, RESPOND WITH ONLY A NUMERIC RATING, FROM 0-10."

S1.3.4 Belief Items

Conspiracy prompt (QID50). Study 2 omitted "If so," and the last two sentences.

"Throughout history, various theories have emerged that suggest certain significant events or situations are the result of secret plans by individuals or groups. These theories often offer alternative explanations for events than those that are widely accepted by the public or presented by official sources. Some people call these 'conspiracy theories'. Reflecting on this, are there any specific such theories that you find particularly credible or compelling? If so, please describe one below and share your reasons for finding it compelling. If not, please explain that you do not believe in any such theories. You will still be able to proceed with the experiment."

Elaboration prompt (QID51): "On the previous question, you wrote: '[QID50 response]'. Could you share more about what led you to find this theory compelling? For instance, are there specific pieces of evidence, events, sources of information, or personal experiences that have particularly influenced your perspective? Please describe these in as much detail as you feel comfortable."

Pretreatment belief rating (0-100 slider): "On the previous question, you wrote about your views in greater detail. We used an artificial intelligence tool to summarize your statement as follows: '[AI-generated single-sentence restatement]'. On a scale of 0% to 100%, please indicate your level of confidence that this statement is true. (NOTE: This question is not about the quality of the summary but rather about your beliefs about the original theory itself)."

Post-treatment belief rating (0-100 slider): "Now that you've had a chance to converse with the AI, we'd like to get back to some of the questions we asked at the beginning of the survey. At the outset of this survey, you suggested that: '[focal conspiracy statement]'. On a scale of 0% to 100%, please indicate your level of confidence that this statement is true." Study 1's explanation prompts (main text) did not force a response.

S1.3.5 Moderator and Individual-Differences Measures

AI familiarity and use (1-7 sliders): "… How much, if anything, have you heard or read about recent advancements in AI such as ChatGPT and Stable Diffusion?" and "How often have you used recent AI tools and services such as ChatGPT and Stable Diffusion? …" Trust, social embeddedness (QID155) and prior exposure: see the main text. Priorexposure item 4 (numbered as in §S5.4) correlates weakly with items 1-3 (r = .07, -.06, .23; four-item α = .43 with it reversed) and is analyzed singly, as is item 2. Figure S2 Exclusion Cascade, Study 2 S2. Data Filtering, Inclusion, and Attrition S2.1 Exclusion Criteria: Preregistered and Additional Both studies preregistered the same exclusion criteria (AsPredicted #174279 and #270259). Participants were excluded if they (a) failed the attention check (one item in Study 1, two in Study 2; §S1.3.5), (b) failed the writing screener ( §S1.3.3), which ran inside the survey before the experiment and so has no step in Figures S1-S2, (c) did not articulate a conspiracy belief according to the classifier ( §S1.3.2), or (d) rated their pretreatment belief below 50.

We also excluded, without preregistration, participants who failed the CAPTCHA, used a mobile device (the chat did not display reliably on mobile browsers), did not reach the AI treatment or had no assigned condition, or gave no post-treatment rating (attrition; §S2.2). In Study 1 we removed duplicate submissions across Samples 1a and 1b (earliest kept), 1 and in Study 2 we excluded participants who flagged the AI summary of their belief as inaccurate (n = 88). The 173 Sample 1a participants in the alternative-model arm ( §S1.1) are excluded from all analyses.

S2.2 Attrition by Condition, Both Studies

Attrition is the share of participants who passed every pretreatment filter and began the treatment but gave no post-treatment rating (Table S2). It was 2.86% in Study 1 and did not differ across conditions. In Study 2 it was 2.80% and was higher in the Baseline treatment than in the other two conditions. Eight of the 26 dropouts (Baseline treatment 5, Control 3) followed a failed first API call that left them without an AI message; without participants whose first call failed, attrition remained higher in the Baseline treatment (3.72%, vs. 0.83% in Single Message and 0.98% in Control; χ²(2, N = 917) = 9.11, p = .011).

1 Sample 1b was recruited from the same pool. When a participant's records from both samples passed every other filter, we kept the earlier one (75 records removed). Another 75 retained records are a participant's second submission whose first had been excluded earlier in the cascade (58 of these participants had already talked with the AI in the alternative-model arm or the non-conspiracy branch). Excluding them (N = 1,215) changes no condition contrast by more than 0.78 points (No Facts vs. Baseline treatment b = 7.91, vs. 7.21 in the full sample).

S2.3 Null-Imputation Sensitivity Analysis (Study 2)

To test whether differential dropout could explain the Study 2 effects, we set posttreatment belief equal to pretreatment belief (i.e., assumed zero treatment effect) for the 26 participants who dropped out and refit the primary model with Control as the reference (N = 929). The Baseline treatment versus Control contrast remained b = -12.51, SE = 1.37, p < .001, and the Single Message versus Control contrast remained b = -10.29, SE = 1.27, p < .001 (Table S3). Differential attrition is therefore unlikely to account for the results.

S4. Primary Treatment Effects: Full Frequentist Statistics, Midpoint Crossing, and Regression-to-the-Mean Checks

p values are not adjusted for multiplicity unless they are labeled Holm-adjusted (Tables S7, S18b, S20, S22).

S4.1 Bayesian Models (Post Hoc)

The preregistered confirmatory analyses are the frequentist regressions in Tables S6 and S8, with the Baseline treatment as reference in both studies. The Bayesian models that organize the main text were not preregistered. They regress belief reduction (pre -post) on condition (reference: Baseline treatment) and centered pretreatment belief (Gaussian likelihood, fit with brms), with weakly informative priors: Normal(0, 20) on regression coefficients, Normal(0, 30) on the intercept and Student-t(3, 0, 20) on the residual SD. Bayes factors are two-sided Savage-Dickey density ratios at zero, reported as BF10 or, for null differences between conditions, as BF01 (evidence for the null). A difference of two coefficients, such as Single Message versus Control in Study 2, has a wider implied prior (Normal(0, 28.3)). When zero lies several posterior SDs from the mean, the density at zero cannot be estimated reliably, and the Bayes factor is reported as "> 1,000".

S4.2 Study 1 -Full Frequentist Contrasts

Realized sensitivity and non-inferiority (post hoc).

Each Study 1 null contrast could detect only large differences: with 80% power, 5.8-6.8 points, or 53-63% of the Baseline treatment's 10.88-point reduction; power to detect a halving of the effect (5.44 points) was .61 to .75. Table S7 reports what the six variants that did not differ from Baseline rule out. All six pass a one-sided non-inferiority test against a half-effect margin (Holm-adjusted p < .05), and the posterior probability that each retains at least half the Baseline effect is ≥ .97. Only for Overt does the 95% interval include a loss of more than half the effect. Two-sided equivalence tests at the same margin fail only for Concise (p = .13) and Abstracted Summary (p = .07), whose estimates lie above Baseline. On average the six variants differ from Baseline by 0.62 points (HC3 95% CI [-2.07, 3.31]). The half-effect margin is lenient, and because Study 1 has no untreated control, the shares are relative to pre-post change, which includes roughly 1.4-1.5 points of untreated drift (Study 2 Control).

S4.3 Study 2 -Full Frequentist Contrasts

Table S8 gives the Study 2 contrasts in the preregistered parameterization (Baseline treatment as reference), with Bayesian and HC3 estimates side by side. The posterior probability that the Baseline treatment reduced belief more than the Single Message is .97 (.71 for an advantage of more than 2 points; .07 for more than 5). The frequentist p for this contrast is .081 with HC3 standard errors, .053 with classical standard errors and .086 by Welch's t on change scores.

S4.4 Midpoint-Crossing Rates

For participants who began above 50, a post-treatment rating below 50 indicates movement from the "true" side of the scale to the "false" side. Participants who began at exactly 50 ("Uncertain"; 53 in Study 1, 4.1%; 36 in Study 2, 4.0%) were not on the believing side of the scale, and because they needed to move only one point to cross they ended below 50 at about three times the rate of other participants in the treatment arms (e.g., Study 1 Baseline treatment 10 of 18; Study 2 Baseline treatment 7 of 12; Single Message 10 of 18). The main text therefore reports the statistic among participants who began above 50; Table S9 gives both versions for every condition. Rates differed by condition in both studies (above-50 starters: Study 1 χ²(7) = 14.11, p = .049, N = 1,237; Study 2 χ²(2) = 43.82, p < .001, N = 867; all participants: χ²(7) = 15.35, p = .032, N = 1,290; χ²(2) = 46.17, p < .001, N = 903). The Study 1 difference is driven by the low rate in No Facts.

S4.5 Regression-to-the-Mean Check

Selecting participants who endorse their focal conspiracy at or above the midpoint creates scope for regression to the mean. Two results argue against it. First, Study 2 Control participants, who met the same entry criterion but talked with the AI about an unrelated topic, reduced their belief by only 1.40 points (Table S8). Second, generalized additive models of belief reduction on pretreatment belief, fit separately for each condition (Figure S4), show reductions in the active conditions across nearly the whole range of pretreatment belief, including among the strongest believers, while the Control and No Facts smooths stay close to zero. Note. (A) Study 1 (N = 1,290). (B) Study 2 (N = 903). Lines are generalized additive model smooths of belief reduction (pre -post; positive values mean that belief decreased) on pretreatment belief, fit separately for each condition; ribbons are pointwise 95% confidence intervals, and points are participants (jittered). The y-axis is limited to -25 to 45 for legibility; 122 Study 1 and 78 Study 2 participants outside this range are included in the fits but not drawn.

S4.6 Prior-SD Sensitivity for Study 1 Bayes Factors

Savage-Dickey Bayes factors depend on the width of the prior on the effect. We therefore refit the Study 1 model with Normal(0, σ β ) priors on the regression coefficients at σ β = 10, 20 (the main models) and 40, all else unchanged (Table S10).

Table S10 Prior-SD Sensitivity, Study 1 Condition vs. Baseline Bayes Factors Condition vs. Baseline σ β = 10 σ β = 20 † σ β = 40 Just-the-Facts (BF01 ) 4.56 9.11 17.70 Concise (BF01 ) 2.11 4.33 8.53 Adversarial (BF01 ) 4.56 8.71 17.44 Overt (BF01 ) 3.52 6.47 13.37 Abstracted Summary (BF01 ) 2.55 5.27 10.06 Belief-First Rating (BF01 ) 4.68 8.79 18.08 No Facts (BF10 ) 38.5 11.4 9.33

Note. †Prior of the main models (Table S6); this column comes from the sensitivity refit and differs from Table S6 by at most 0.05 (sampling and density-estimation error).

The pattern does not depend on prior width: every variant other than No Facts yields BF01 > 1, and No Facts yields evidence for a difference (BF10 from 9.3 to 38.5), at every width. The weakest null contrast is Concise at σ β = 10 (BF01 = 2.11), consistent with its point estimate of 2.7 points more reduction than Baseline (Table S6).

S5. Moderators of the Treatment Effect

Trust in generative AI was measured before treatment in both studies, and social embeddedness and prior exposure to counterarguments in Study 2 ( §S1.3.5); actively openminded thinking (AOT) comes from Costello et al. (2024; §S5.3). Belief importance, pretreatment belief, conspiracy topic and demographic moderators are in §S5.5.

The main text reports every interaction on the belief-reduction (pre -post) scale, where a positive coefficient means a larger treatment effect at higher values of the moderator, as do Tables S11, S12, S18a and S18b. Tables S13-S17 model post-treatment belief or belief change (post -pre), where a negative coefficient means a larger treatment effect. In Tables S13, S15 and S16 S18a. The per-scale-point embeddedness, prior-exposure, item 2, item 4 and Study 2 trust fits, the AOT fit and the fixed-effects-only pooled trust fit use the priors of §S4.1 except Normal(50, 30) on the intercept; the other per-SD refits in Tables S18a and S18b use the priors of §S4.1 unchanged.

S5.1 Trust in Generative AI (Pooled Analysis)

We pooled Study 2 with the two treatment-versus-control experiments of Costello et al. (2024), each of which contributes its own randomly assigned control and treatment arms (pooled N = 2,594, 897 control and 1,697 treatment; arm sizes in Table S11). Only the Baseline treatment arm of Study 2 is coded as treatment; the Single Message arm (n = 364) is excluded because it has no analogue in the Costello et al. (2024) experiments. Trust in generative AI is the same single item in all three experiments ( §S1.3.5), centered within experiment. The pooled model is stratified by experiment: belief reduction (pre -post) is regressed on pretreatment belief, experiment fixed effects, experiment-specific treatment effects, experiment-specific trust slopes and a common treatment × trust interaction, so that every treatment-control contrast is made within an experiment.

Table S11 reports this model with HC3-robust standard errors and as the Bayesian model of the main text (priors as in §S4.1; treatment × trust posterior mean 2.13, 95% CrI [1.09, 3.16], BF10 > 30). The posterior treatment effect was 10.7 [8.3, 13.1] points at 1 SD below mean trust (SD = 1.58), 14.1 [12.4, 15.7] at the mean and 17.4 [15.1, 19.7] at 1 SD above. The interaction barely changes with experiment fixed effects only (2.06, 95% CrI [1.04, 3.08], BF10 = 28.7) or with the two data-collection waves of Costello et al. (2024, Study 2) as separate strata (2.10 [1.25, 2.95]).

S5.2 Social Embeddedness (Study 2)

Social embeddedness is a single 0-100 slider ( §S1.3.5). Table S13 reports the linear models; its note gives a generalized additive model (GAM) with a smooth of embeddedness in each condition. In the Bayesian Baseline-treatment-versus-Control model the interaction is -0.065 points of belief reduction per slider point (95% CrI [-0.188, 0.060]). Its Bayes factor depends on the moderator's scale under the Normal(0, 20) prior: BF10 = 0.005 per slider point and 0.15 per SD (Table S18a).

S5.3 Actively Open-Minded Thinking (AOT) -Costello et al. (2024) Follow-Up

AOT was not measured in the present studies. The analysis in the main text (Figure 3C) uses Study 1 of Costello et al. (2024), whose participants completed the six-item AOT subscale (1-6, all items reverse-scored; α = .91, M = 4.11, SD = 1.05) at a follow-up a median of 12.0 days after the experiment (IQR 11.8-12.3). AOT was therefore measured after treatment, in both arms. The sample is participants with pretreatment belief ≥ 50 and complete belief and AOT data (N = 623; 258 control, 365 treatment). Post-treatment belief was regressed on centered pretreatment belief, condition, standardized AOT and condition × AOT (Table S14; negative coefficients mean larger reductions). A 1-SD increase in AOT went with a 3.73-point larger treatment effect (HC3 p = .009); the Bayesian companion gives 3.72, 95% CrI [0.54, 6.95], BF10 = 1.09; the Bayes factor is equivocal, although the credible interval excludes zero. Simple effects at ±1 SD are in Table S18a; control-arm belief change was essentially flat across AOT.

Because AOT was measured after treatment, participants who had been persuaded may afterwards have described themselves as more open-minded. Two checks make that reading less likely without excluding it: follow-up AOT did not differ by condition (treatment M = 4.13, control M = 4.08; d = 0.04, 95% CI [-0.12, 0.20], p = .605), and follow-up completion did not differ across arms (365 of 468 vs. 258 of 328 participants with pretreatment belief ≥ 50; 78.0% vs. 78.7%, p = .822) and was unrelated to belief change (arm × reduction interaction p = .49).

Table S14 AOT Moderation (Costello et al. 2024 Follow-Up; N = 623) Term b SE t p 95% CI (Intercept) 82.96 0.56 149.04 < .001 [81.87, 84.05] Pretreatment belief (centered) 0.927 0.055 16.74 < .001 [0.819, 1.036] Condition (Treatment vs. Control) -16.03 1.43 -11.21 < .001 [-18.84, -13.23] AOT (standardized) -0.70 0.63 -1.11 .265 [-1.95, 0.54] Condition × AOT -3.73 1.41 -2.64 .009 [-6.51, -0.96]

S5.4 Prior Exposure to Counterarguments (Study 2)

The prior-exposure composite is the mean of items 1 ("I have heard many arguments against this belief before") and 3 ("I have discussed this belief with people who disagreed with me"; r = .45, α = .62), the two items that directly indicate exposure to counterarguments ( §S1.3.5). Items 2 and 4 measure other things and are analyzed alone ( § §S5.4a-S5.4b). Neither interaction with the composite approached significance (Table S15). The Bayesian Baselinetreatment-versus-Control interaction is -0.63 points of belief reduction per scale point (95% CrI [-3.56, 2.27]), BF10 = 0.08 (BF01 ≈ 12), and the Baseline treatment effect was similar at low and high exposure (Table S18a).

One might expect participants who had already heard many counterarguments to respond less, because they would already know some of what the AI says. They did not. The composite records how much counterargument people recall, not its quality: what people meet on their own is usually generic and scattered, whereas the AI's counterevidence is detailed and tailored. What matters may also be counterevidence that is new to the person, which recalled exposure captures only weakly; the present measures cannot separate these readings.

Table S15 Prior-Exposure Moderation (Study 2, Composite of Items 1 + 3) Term b SE t p (Intercept) -1.41 0.70 -2.02 .044 Pretreatment belief (centered) -0.113 0.045 -2.51 .012 Full Dialogue (vs. Control) -13.14 1.42 -9.26 < .001 Single Message (vs. Control) -10.35 1.29 -8.03 < .001 Prior exposure (centered) +0.169 0.69 0.25 .806 Full Dialogue × Prior Exposure +0.628 1.25 0.50 .617 Single Message × Prior Exposure +0.304 1.25 0.24 .808 Note. The item-level tests (Table S16; Figure S5) are exploratory: the eight item × condition terms are jointly not significant, none survives Holm adjustment ( §S5.4a), and the Bayes factors for the composite and for items 2 and 4 are all below 1.

S5.4a Item 2 Standalone -"I Have Seriously Considered Whether This Belief Might Be Wrong"

Item 2 reports past reflection on the belief rather than exposure to counterarguments (it correlates r = .35 and .30 with items 1 and 3, r = -.06 with item 4, and r = -.25 with pretreatment belief). It was answered before random assignment, on the same page as the pretreatment belief rating ( §S1.2), and its means are balanced across arms (Control 3.50, Baseline treatment 3.38, Single Message 3.48; p = .48). The treatment therefore cannot have influenced it, but the evidence that it moderates the treatment is inconclusive. In the Bayesian Baseline-treatment-versus-Control model (N = 539), the item × treatment interaction is 2.08 points of additional belief reduction per scale point, 95% CrI [-0.35, 4.57], BF10 = 0.25 (BF01 = 4.0; posterior probability of a positive interaction = .95). The HC3 test is nominally significant (2.06, 95% CI [0.05, 4.08], p = .045), but p = .31 after Holm adjustment across the eight item tests, .063 with a treatment × pretreatment-belief term added and .10 with classical standard errors. The Baseline treatment effect was substantial at every level of the item, from 8.4 points (95% CI [2.9, 13.9]) among participants who strongly disagreed to 16.7 [12.3, 21.0] among those who strongly agreed (±1 SD in Table S18a). The point estimate is in the direction predicted by the actively open-minded thinking result ( §S5.3), but the credible interval includes zero and the Bayes factor favors the null, so it neither confirms nor refutes that result.

S5.4b Item 4 Standalone -"The Arguments I've Heard Against This Belief Were Weak or Unconvincing"

Item 4 is the largest moderator among the four prior-exposure items (Table S16). Per scale point, a higher score predicts 1.68 points less belief reduction in Control (HC3 p = .014) and a further 3.06 points less under the Baseline treatment (p = .017; Table S16, post -pre scale). Per SD and on the reduction scale, the interaction is -3.51 [-6.39, -0.64], the value in the main text (Tables S18a-S18b). The treatment still worked for participants high on the item, only less well (9.4 points relative to Control at 1 SD above the mean, 95% CI [5.8, 13.0]). The Bayes factor is equivocal (BF10 = 0.77 per scale point and 0.84 per SD; BF01 = 1.3 per scale point), although the credible interval narrowly excludes zero.

Agreeing that past counterarguments were weak could reflect a belief closer to the truth, for which counterarguments really are weaker; a general tendency to discount counterevidence; poor sources met in the past; or other correlated dispositions. The item's near-zero correlation with item 2 ( §S5.4a) argues mildly against a simple low-reflection account but does not separate the others, so we do not commit to an interpretation. Note. Posterior Baseline treatment -Control difference in belief reduction as a function of (A) the prior-exposure composite (mean of items 1 and 3), (B) item 4 and (C) item 2, each rated 1-5. Lines are posterior medians, bands are 95% credible intervals, and gray strips show each moderator's distribution; numbers give the difference at 1 SD below and above the moderator mean. BF10 is for the treatment × moderator interaction per scale point under a Normal(0, 20) prior.

Tables S18a and S18b report the remaining moderation tests, all post hoc. Table S18a gives each focal moderator's Baseline treatment × Moderator interaction from the three-arm Study 2 model, with the treatment effect at 1 SD below and above the moderator mean. Table S18b adds Holm-adjusted p values, the Single Message terms, joint tests, the demographic and topic moderators, Study 1 associations and the corresponding randomized tests in Costello et al. (2024).

Belief importance.

In Study 2, the Baseline treatment effect was directionally smaller, though still large, for more important beliefs (interaction p = .112). Among Study 2 participants rating the belief "very" to "extremely" important (6-8 on the 0-8 scale; n = 267, 56 in Control), the Baseline treatment reduced belief by 9.80 points relative to Control (95% CI [5.24, 14.35]), compared with 14.68 [11.24, 18.12] among the rest (n = 636). The same attenuation is significant in Study 2 of Costello et al. (2024) (p = .001) and appears as an association in the present Study 1, which has no control arm (Table S18b). In Study 1 it is specific to the evidence-based arms (No Facts × Importance +1.19 per point, 95% CI [0.22, 2.16], p = .017): the No Facts effect was near zero among participants rating the belief very to extremely important (1.58 points, 95% CI [-4.42, 7.57], n = 33), so the Study 1 figures use the other seven arms.

Conspiracy topic.

Belief reductions among treated participants differed across topics ( §S8.3). The randomized Study 2 test asks a different question, whether the Baseline treatment's advantage over Control differs across topics, and it was not significant (Baseline treatment × topic F(7, 878) = 1.25, p = .274; the effect was positive in all eight topic groups). This does not show equal effects: cells are small (10-28 Control and 8-35 Baseline participants per named topic), and the Single Message × topic test was significant with this grouping (p = .040, unadjusted; other groupings in the Table S18b

note).

Gender. Gender is the only demographic moderator with p < .05 in Study 2 (women minus men +8.34 points; n = 885, participants of other or unreported gender excluded). The Baseline treatment effect was 8.94 points [4.97, 12.90] among men and 17. 28 [13.34, 21.22] among women. The difference survives Holm adjustment across the 12 Study 2 tests in Table S18b (p = .042) but does not appear in Study 1 or in Costello et al. (2024), and we do not interpret it.

Single Message.

Importance and social embeddedness significantly moderate the Single Message effect (unadjusted p = .004 and .011) but not the Baseline treatment effect (Table S18b), although the two arms' interactions do not differ significantly from each other (importance p = .378; embeddedness p = .267, Table S13).
