The omnibus topic test has no single coefficient and its Bayes factor depends strongly on the prior, so none is given.

S5.6 Correlations Among Pretreatment Moderators

Figure S6 shows the correlations among the Study 2 pretreatment moderators, pretreatment belief, importance and demographics. They are modest. The strongest are between pretreatment belief and importance (r = .41) and between social embeddedness and both importance and pretreatment belief (r = .34 each). Trust in generative AI correlates weakly with every other variable (all |r| ≤ .21), so the pooled trust moderation (Table S11) is unlikely to reflect overlap with the other moderators. Note. Pearson correlations, pairwise complete observations (N = 903). Prior exposure is the composite of items 1 and 3.

S6. Persuasion Strategy Annotation and Mediation Model

S6. Persuasion Strategy Annotation and Mediation Model

S6.1 GPT-4o Annotation Pipeline

The 16 persuasive strategies (Table S19) come from the analysis code of Costello et al. (2024), where they were the strategies named in at least half of 10 independent queries asking GPT-4 Turbo how it would talk someone out of a conspiracy belief. Table S19 gives the definitions used in our annotation prompt. Costello et al. (2024) rated all strategies in one GPT-4 Turbo prompt; we rated each strategy in a separate call, with shortened definitions, an added "Uncertain" option and a rating for each of the three AI turns.

For each conversation and strategy, GPT-4o (gpt-4o-2024-08-06, temperature 0) rated the AI's use of the strategy in each round and returned the ratings as JSON. All 1,401 rated conversations (every Study 1 arm, including participants later excluded from the analytic sample) were coded. "Uncertain" was returned for 14 of the 22,416 first-round ratings (0.06%). Only the first-round ratings (the AI's first message) enter the PCA, the prompt contrasts, the GAM and the mediation model. A simplified excerpt of the prompt follows; in it the AI is the "Debunker" and the participant the "Believer".

"You are an expert annotator analyzing persuasion strategies in conversations on conspiracy theories. You will see a written conversation between two individuals: a Debunker and a Believer. The Debunker's role is to convince the Believer that their conspiracy theory is incorrect. Each conversation has 3 rounds. Table S19 Definitions of 16 Persuasive Strategies Used to Prompt GPT-4o Strategy Definition Alternative Explanations Provide plausible, evidence-based alternative perspectives or explanations for events attributed to conspiracy theories. Analogies / Metaphors Use analogies or metaphors to simplify complex information or draw parallels. Build Rapport Establish a respectful and understanding relationship with the Believer. Common Ground Identify and build on shared beliefs or values. Conflicting Evidence Introduce facts that contradict claims made by the conspiracy theory. Credible Sources Introduce information from reliable sources. Critical Thinking Encourage the Believer to question the logic and sources behind their beliefs. Encourage Empathy Help the Believer consider the impact of beliefs on others. Expert Consensus Emphasize agreement among credible authorities. Harm Discuss the personal or societal harms of conspiracy beliefs. Inconsistencies / Fallacies Identify logical inconsistencies or fallacies in arguments. Maintaining Patience Demonstrate patience in engaging in gradual dialogue. Offer Resources Provide resources for further learning or alternative perspectives. Psychological Needs Address emotional aspects or psychological needs related to beliefs. Socratic Questioning Use a questioning approach to examine beliefs. Stories / Examples Share stories, anecdotes, or examples.

S6.2 Polychoric PCA of 16 Strategies → 5 Components

First-round ratings were scored None = 0, Low = 1, Moderate = 2, High = 3. On their polychoric correlations, parallel analysis indicated five components, and we fit a varimax-rotated PCA with five components (loadings in Figure 4A). Component scores are regression scores from the rotated solution, not averages of the strategies loading on each component.

Two fits are used. The loadings in Figure 4A and the scores in Figure 4B and Table S20 come from all 1,366 coded conversations with complete first-round ratings and pre-and posttreatment beliefs, including participants outside the analytic sample. The GAM, the mediation model and Table S25 use a second fit on the analytic-sample conversations. The two solutions agree closely for four components (score correlations .90-.96) and less well for Stories / Metaphors / Examples (.51), on which Socratic Questioning loads more heavily in the analyticsample fit.

Prompt contrasts (Figure 4B, Table S20). The contrasts use the four conditions as randomized, in the analytic sample (N = 659; the mediation model in §S6.4 uses N = 658). Scores are z-scored within this frame, so an SD includes between-condition variance and a difference in SD units understates a within-condition Cohen's d. Table S20 reports all 30 pairwise contrasts. The five Study 1 arms that share the Baseline system prompt do not differ on the component scores (analytic sample; all p > .12).

In the four-arm frame, the No Facts elevation on Harms & Empathy comes from Encourage Empathy (rated present in 91% of No Facts first messages vs. 54% in the Baseline treatment; M = 2.26 vs. 0.75 on the 0-3 scale), not from Harm, which was rarer in No Facts (19% vs. 33%, p = .002; M = 0.26 vs. 0.46, p = .003). About half of the No Facts first messages rated at least Low on Harm dwell on the human toll of the event in question and about half on what the belief costs the believer; explicit discussion of the harms of conspiracy beliefs was uncommon in every condition. No Facts almost never cited sources or expert consensus

S6.3 Generalized Additive Model (GAM)

The GAM (Figure 5) regressed belief change on smooth terms for the five component scores and centered pretreatment belief (R package mgcv; smoothness chosen by generalized cross-validation), without adjusting for condition.

Facts & Evidence (edf = 2.83) and Sources & Experts (edf = 1.29) had positive, roughly monotonic associations with belief reduction (both p < .001), and Harms & Empathy a small negative, roughly linear one (edf = 1.00, p = .001). Rapport / Common Ground (edf = 3.48, p = .31) and Stories / Metaphors / Examples (edf = 1.00, p = .50) were not significantly associated with belief change. The mediation model, which adjusts for condition, finds no significant association for Harms & Empathy, Rapport or Stories (Table S21).

S6.4 Multiple Mediation Structural Equation Model

The path model (R package lavaan; specified in the main text) uses the four Study 1 conditions whose prompts changed the AI's strategy mix (Baseline treatment as the reference; N = 658 with complete component scores), with centered pretreatment belief as a covariate in every equation. Component scores are in SD units (standardized in the 1,174 rated conversations), so the five b-paths share a scale.

Of the No Facts total indirect association with belief change (-15.96), the Facts & Evidence path accounts for 69% (-11.07) and the Sources & Experts path for 20% (-3.14). These shares are not proportions mediated, because the direct effect of No Facts was positive (7.86, p = .057) against a total effect of -8.10. Because No Facts lies 2.3 SD below the Baseline treatment on Facts & Evidence, its indirect path also largely extrapolates a within-condition slope. The total indirect paths of Just-the-Facts and Concise were near zero.

Table S22 tests the differences among the five b-paths with the same bootstrap resamples. Each evidence coefficient exceeds each of the other three without adjustment; after Holm adjustment over the 10 pairs, Sources & Experts vs. Rapport / Common Ground does not (p = .10). Strategy use was measured, not randomized, so these associations are consistent with mediation but do not demonstrate it; the pairwise contrasts were not preregistered.

Study 1 yielded 30,662 claim instances (30,314 distinct texts) from 1,238 conversations, and Study 2 yielded 27,535 (27,352 distinct) from 888. Each distinct text was classified once, but every instance counts in the per-conversation counts.

S7.2 Six-Category Claim Taxonomy and Codebook

Gemini 3 Flash (Google) assigned each distinct claim one of 30 subtypes, given the subtype definitions with two examples each and no conversational context; the six top-level categories are derived from the subtypes ( §S7.7). Table S23 gives the top-level definitions, with verbatim example claims drawn from the top quartile of the classifier's confidence ratings.

Table S23 Six-Category Claim Taxonomy Category (subtypes) Definition Example claims (assigned subtype) Specific empirical (SE1-SE6) Concrete, checkable claims about events, records, actors, procedures, findings, documents, or physical evidence. "Operation Northwoods was a 1962 Pentagon proposal involving staged incidents." (Historical events and documented precedent) / "The Apollo flag had a horizontal rod along the top edge to hold it out." (Physical / forensic / visual evidence) Inferentiallogical (IL1-IL6) Object-level reasoning about what would follow if the conspiracy were true, what evidence should exist, how coordination would work, or why a mundane alternative is more plausible. "It would require keeping a secret amongst thousands of people for over 50 years if the moon landing were faked." (Coordination / leak-risk logic) / "If there were alien craft or bodies, you would expect consistent documentation that survives scrutiny." (Necessary-evidence-if-true reasoning) Meta-epistemic (ME1-ME3) Claims about how one should evaluate evidence, compare sources, apply standards of proof, or reason under uncertainty.

"Verified evidence of alien life would require evidence that clearly cannot be explained by anything else and can be checked by many independent experts." (Evidencestandard norm) / "The scientific method involves hypothesis testing, evidence gathering, and peer review." (Reliability / method guidance) Moral / political evaluation (MP1-MP3) Moral, civic, political, or prudential evaluations about power, justice, abuse, policy risk, empathy, or public consequences.

"In democracies, the electorate is the ultimate check on government." (Civic / prosocial value) / "If exposed, allowing such an attack would be one of the biggest crimes in US history." (Moral / justice evaluation) Minimal / nonadjudicative (LI1 -LI5) Definitional, tautological, banal, minimally informative, or merely background statements that do little adjudicative work. "Logical fallacies are errors in reasoning that weaken arguments." (Definitional clarification) / "Parkland Hospital is the same hospital where Kennedy and later Oswald were taken." (Minimal event/background fact) General / underspecified (GU1-GU7) Broad, hedged, contextual, or underspecified claims that are not precise enough to verify as written. "Most evidence for aliens at Area 51 is anecdotal and hard to verify." (Absence-of-clear-evidence framing) / "Governments do hide things, mostly for military reasons." (Secrecy / protection / cover-up possibility framing) S7.3 Claim Composition by Condition -Study 1 and Study 2 Table S24 Claim Volume and Composition by Condition Condition Conversations (in analytic S7.4 Claim-Level Association With Belief Change

Pooled OLS models regressed belief reduction on the within-conversation share of one claim type at a time, controlling for pretreatment belief, study and conspiracy topic (fixed effects; HC3 standard errors). A larger Inferential-logical share predicted larger reductions and a mostly false focal belief, 19.1% a mixed, 7.5% a plausible and 3.1% a too-vague one; of the 903 in Study 2, 73.4%, 15.1%, 10.4% and 1.1%. Table S27 gives claim composition by veracity label, computed in Python (pandas, statsmodels).

S7.7 Granular Claim Subtypes

The codebook was developed after extraction and was not preregistered. An initial fivecategory classification mixed evidence-standard guidance with moral and political evaluation and labeled many broad empirical claims as unfalsifiable, so we split it into six categories and 30 subtypes, which label the rows of Figure S7. Their definitions are in the codebook file in the replication package (data/codebooks/study2_claim_category_v2_codebook.csv).

S8. Conspiracy Topic Analysis (HDBSCAN)

S8. Conspiracy Topic Analysis (HDBSCAN)

S8.1 Embedding + HDBSCAN Clustering Procedure

We embedded each participant's AI-generated one-sentence restatement of their focal belief with OpenAI's text-embedding-3-large model, reduced the embeddings to 30 principal components (48% of the variance), and clustered them with HDBSCAN, varying the minimum cluster size (minPts) from 10 to 25 (Table S28). The grid was ranked by a score that favored 8-12 topics, but those solutions merged distinct themes (e.g., Mandela Effect, Mass Surveillance, Chemtrails) into broad clusters. We therefore used the 18-topic solution at minPts = 10 (noise fraction = 0.41); the 8-12-topic solutions gave similar topic-level rankings on the topics they kept. Re-embedding can shift cluster boundaries slightly, so all analyses use the cached cluster assignments distributed with the code ( §S11).

Retained clusters have at least 10 members. CV = coefficient of variation of retainedcluster sizes; study segregation = how strongly clusters separate Study 1 from Study 2 participants (lower is better). Solutions with 8-12 retained clusters are ranked first, by noise fraction and then study segregation; ranks 10-15 are omitted (full grid in the replication materials, §S11). S8.3 Topic-Level Belief Reductions

We pooled the evidence-based conditions of both studies (all Study 1 conditions except No Facts; Study 2 Baseline treatment and Single Message) and regressed belief reduction on topic, controlling for study, for topics with at least 15 participants (N = 1,807). Belief reductions differed across topics (Wald χ²(14) = 63.1, p < .001; Figure S9B), in each study separately (both ps < .01) and after adjusting for pretreatment belief (p < .001). They were largest for Moon Landing Hoax (M adj = 20.55), 9/11 Inside Job (17.52) and Secret Elite Control (16.22), and positive for all but two of the 18 clusters (MKUltra, -0.58, n = 17; COVID Vaccine Harms, raw mean -5.64, n = 14, below the reporting threshold). In these conditions, the clustering file includes 90 participants outside the analytic samples and lacks 100 analytic Study 1 participants; among analytic participants only, the test is χ²(14) = 60.9, p < .001 (N = 1,721), and 16 of 18 clusters remain positive. These are descriptive differences among treated participants, with no control comparison. Of the present experiments, only Study 2 can test whether the treatment effect itself differs by topic, because Study 1 has no control arm; there, the randomized Baseline treatment × topic test is not significant (p = .274; §S5.5, Table S18b), as in Costello et al. (2024; F(12, 1971) = 1.30, p = .21).

S8.4 Focal-Conspiracy-Veracity Subset Sensitivity

We re-estimated the Study 1 condition effects (Table S6) and the Study 2 contrasts with Control (Table S8) among participants whose focal conspiracy was labeled false or mostly false ( §S7.6), which removes beliefs that are arguably partly true. The treatment effects were, if anything, slightly larger in this subset, and the No Facts condition's ineffectiveness was more pronounced (Tables S30a and S30b); Figure S8 shows belief reduction by condition for each veracity label.

S9.1 Codebook Development and GPT-4o Coding Pipeline

To quantify Study 1 participants' explanations of why they did or did not find the AI persuasive ( §S1.3.4), we developed, post hoc, 15 attributes (Table S31).

The attributes were developed inductively. One author read the responses and listed the recurring themes; an AI model, given the responses, independently produced a list of candidate categories; and the two lists were merged into one taxonomy of six positive (appreciative, "+") and nine negative (critical, "-") attributes.

GPT-4o (gpt-4o-2024-08-06, temperature 0) coded one attribute per call, for each response, with the prompt below. The bracketed fields were filled with the Table S31 definitions, the attribute being coded, the question the participant had answered, and whether their belief had fallen ("succeeded") or not ("failed"); the condition was not given.

"You are provided with a list of participant responses regarding why a persuasive conversation with an AI either succeeded or failed in changing their beliefs. Your role is to annotate each response as an expert coder. Although you are coding one theme for this task, please keep in mind the definitions of all the following categories: [List of categories and definitions] Today, your focus is on the following theme: [Name and definition] Note: Themes with a '+' in their name (e.g., EVID+, EMPH+) should be coded when the participant praises the AI's attributes. Themes with a '-' in their name (e.g., LACK-, BIAS-) should be coded when the participant criticizes the AI's persuasive qualities.

In this case, the AI was determined to have [succeed_or_failed] in changing beliefs. The participant's response was provided in answer to the following question: [posttreatment question]. The response itself will be piped in as the first user response.

Instructions: 1. Read the participant's response carefully. 2. Decide if the theme defined above applies. If it does, output 1; if it does not, output 0.3. If you are unsure, err on the side of caution and output 0. That is, AVOID FALSE POSITIVES. Output your answer strictly in JSON format exactly as follows (do not include any extra text, only 0 or 1): [JSON instructions]." Table S31 Definitions of the 15 Participant-Account Attributes Attribute (short code) Definition Evidence / Facts / Logic / Counterarguments (EVID+)

The respondent (a) mentions or alludes to concrete data, statistics, facts, and/or (b) mentions logic, rationality, legibility, or appeals to common sense, and/or (c) notes the AI addressed or anticipated their doubts, questions, or alternative views. Expertise / Credibility / Trustworthiness (EXPT+)

The respondent (a) describes the AI as knowledgeable, authoritative, or expert-like and/or (b) as a reliable, unbiased, or "neutral" source of information and/or (c) values the AI's reliance on trustworthy sources and/or (d) views the AI as having unique or impressive access to what is true by dint of being an AI. Empathy / Politeness / Respectfulness (EMPH+)

The respondent (a) highlights that the AI was nonjudgmental, supportive, understood their feelings, or validated their viewpoint -even if it disagreed -and/or (b) calls out the AI's polite, kind, or courteous manner and/or (c) mentions feeling calmer, relieved, or emotionally supported by the AI. Detailed / In-Depth / Novel / Tailored (DETL+)

The respondent (a) praises the AI for being thorough, detailed, or in-depth and/or (b) mentions that the AI introduced new perspectives, angles, or data they hadn't considered and/or (c) that the AI's response was multi-faceted or point-by-point, covering the topic thoroughly and/or (d) that the AI's response was personalized, tailored to their specific questions.

Conspiracy-Specific Mechanisms (CONX+)

The respondent (a) describes specific mechanisms or details of the conspiracy theory, such as how the building's structure led to collapse, how UFO sightings are often secret aircraft, or forensic details of the JFK case, and/or (b) mentioned a meta-point relevant to conspiracy theories, like that large conspiracies would be impossible to hide since someone would have "spilled the beans". Privacy (PRIV+)

The respondent felt they could ask the AI questions without fear of judgment or repercussions. Perceived lack of AI disagreement (LACK-)

The respondent indicates that they already held the belief in question and that the AI merely reaffirmed what they already believed. Repetition / Regurgitation / Lack of Novelty / Lack of Deep Engagement (DEEP-)

The respondent criticizes the AI for repeating well-known or familiar arguments, offering nothing new beyond what they'd heard before and/or the AI is seen as failing to challenge or engage with the respondent's specific points -merely echoing them rather than providing a substantive counterpoint. Offensiveness / Anger (ANGR-)

The respondent appears angry or upset about the conversation.

Insufficient Evidence / Lack of Concrete Data / No Sourcing (EVID-)

The respondent argues that the AI's response lacked hard data, concrete evidence, or sources to back up its claims.

Perceived Bias (BIAS-)

The respondent perceives that the AI's arguments are skewed toward a particular narrative and fail to engage alternative viewpoints. Impersonal Tone / Verbosity / Lack of Nuance (MECH-)

The response is critiqued for being too long-winded, mechanical, or overly formal -lacking the human touch or nuance that might otherwise engage the reader.

Distrust in AI (TRST-)

The respondent expresses skepticism toward the idea that an AI -due to its machine nature, limitations, or potential biases -can be a convincing or trustworthy source. Off-Topic / Superficial Engagement (OFFT-)

The respondent notes that the AI's responses stray from addressing the core issue or are too general, failing to target the specific points of the debate.

Emotional / Experiential Disconnect (EMOT-)

The respondent finds that the AI's arguments lack a personal, emotional, or experiential dimension, making the discussion feel disconnected from real human concerns.

S9.2 Qualitative Observations and Selected Illustrative Quotes

Participants whose belief fell most often said that the AI gave them new facts or evidence and argued logically and clearly. Some also described its tone as polite, patient, or nonconfrontational, and some valued that it acknowledged their concerns while refuting them or answered their points one by one. About 5% mentioned the argument that a large conspiracy would be impossible to hide. Among participants whose belief did not fall, some said they already agreed with the AI, although it had in fact disagreed with them. Others said it did not engage with their points ("Made oppositional claims without comprehending my points and argument fully"), strayed off topic (mostly in No Facts: "The AI didn't really address my questions, it kind of just reverted back to its message of not questioning the unknown."), repeated itself, or gave no sources ("I wanted their sources. … I didn't want similar. I wanted their sources."), often with a charge of bias toward an "official story." Some rejected AI as a source ("Because I have zero trust in AI, and it's not something we should be messing with. This technology will kill us all."), and some found it cold, textbook-like, or blind to emotional and lived experience ("They felt a little cold. … it felt like I was reading words from a textbook rather than having a conversation.").

S9.3 Quantitative Breakdown -Persuaded vs. Unpersuaded

Figure 6 is based on 1,058 participants (belief reduced, n = 584; unchanged or increased, n = 474). Codes are not mutually exclusive; Figure S10 shows how many codes each explanation received and which attributes were mentioned together.

Off-pattern codes. Some positive attributes appear among unchanged-or-increased participants, and some criticisms among belief-reduced participants; they are kept as coded. Because codes overlap, we count them by participant (11.2% and 15.1%) rather than by summing bars. Reading these responses, most are mixed or contrary appraisals: praise with a caveat, participants whose belief fell slightly but who deny having been persuaded, and unchanged participants who say the AI was persuasive or report a slider error. Criticisms became rarer as the belief reduction grew. The same reading showed recurrent coding errors: CONX+ applied when participants restated their own theory, EVID+ for negated mentions of evidence, LACK-for "I agree with the AI", and EVID-when the evidence said to be lacking is the conspiracy's. These errors concentrate in the small off-pattern cells.

Known-groups check.

Although the coder was not told the condition, its codes separated No Facts from the other arms in the expected direction (analytic participants with codes, n = 1,177, of whom 116 No Facts): insufficient evidence (EVID-) 18.1% vs. 7.3% (OR = 2.82, Fisher p < .001); off-topic or superficial engagement (OFFT-) 24.1% vs. 4.3% (OR = 7.00, p < .001); evidence, facts and logic (EVID+) 5.2% vs. 34.5% (OR = 0.10, p < .001); perceived lack of disagreement (LACK-) 12.9% vs. 7.5% (p = .048). DEEP-, BIAS-and TRST-did not differ (ps ≥ .26). Note. (A) Percentage of explanations in each group coded with 0, 1, 2, 3, or 4 or more of the 15 attributes. (B) Phi coefficients (φ, the correlation between two present/absent codes) for every pair of attributes coded in at least 3% of the group's explanations, ordered by hierarchical clustering; each label sits at the right end of its row and the top of its column. Red cells mark pairs mentioned together more often than the two attributes' separate frequencies imply, and blue cells less often; printed values give the sign. (+) and (-) mark positive and negative attributes (Table S31). Frame as in Figure 6 (N = 1,058).

S10. Additional Robustness Checks and Covariate-Adjusted Model

S10. Additional Robustness Checks and Covariate-Adjusted Model

S10.1 Covariate-Adjusted Main-Effects Model (Study 2)

The model in Table S32 regresses Study 2 belief reduction (pre -post) on condition and the pretreatment covariates and moderators listed in the table. Because only pretreatment variables enter, the condition effects are not biased by controlling for consequences of treatment. The model uses Study 2 only, because no participant was randomized between a Study 1 arm and the Study 2 Control. It is a main-effects model, not a test of moderation (for which see §S5 and Table S18b).

The excerpts illustrate the strategy profile in §S6.2 (Table S20): fewer facts and sources, and more stories, metaphors and appeals to empathy, than in the Baseline treatment.

Table S33 Representative Excerpts From the AI's First Response in the No Facts Condition # Focal conspiracy Pre → Post AI excerpt (first turn) 1 The government is concealing the full truth about UFO sightings. 85 → 10 "It's kind of amazing to think about the possibility of not being alone in the universe… have you ever noticed how sometimes stories and mysteries can grow bigger and more intriguing as they're told and retold? Consider, for a moment, the impact of stories and narratives on our lives. Stories help us make sense of the world around us; they fill in the gaps when things seem uncertain or incomplete…" 10 The Rothschilds and Rockefellers secretly control global affairs. 100 → 99 "But let's focus on a different perspective for a moment, without diving into debates or trying to dismantle beliefs with heavy facts or challenging arguments… It's an intricate tapestry of humanity, with each thread interwoven not by the hands of a few but by the collective actions of all of us…" 11 Cloud seeding / chemtrails are being employed as weather modification. 67 → 66 "Did you know that the world of weather modification, especially when it comes to practices like cloud seeding, is so much more mundane and less mysterious than it seems? … There's a certain beauty in the simplicity and transparency of genuine, honest efforts to manage resources and support our planet." 12 The Elite intend to reduce global population, using COVID-19 as a tool. 100 → 100 "But imagine for a moment that things might not be as dire as they appear… Fear can make us see connections and intentions that aren't really there, kind of like how shadows can look like monsters in the dark until we turn on the light…" 13 Jeffrey Epstein's death was a homicide or staged, not a suicide. 100 → 100 "When we think about Epstein and the events surrounding his death, it's easy to be drawn into the mystery and intrigue because it resonates with our desire for justice and our fear of unchecked power… Sometimes, leaning into our emotions and acknowledging how a situation makes us feel can be more revealing than we expect…" 14 The destruction of the Twin Towers on 9/11 was orchestrated by the US Government. 60 → 60 "I truly believe the idea of 9/11 being an inside job isn't supported, and here's a gentle way to look at it: imagine the immense heartache and loss that day brought to so many families, friends, and loved ones… Letting go of unsupported claims helps us respect and remember them in a way that dignifies their sacrifice and bravery ."

15 Netanyahu intentionally allowed the Hamas attack to perpetuate war on Palestine. 95 → 95 "I want to share a perspective with you -not arguing against what you've heard or asking you to change your mind based on facts or evidence, but rather inviting you to consider another angle… Think about the human element involved -the individuals, families, and communities on every side who are caught in an incredibly difficult situation." 16 The government and pharma are concealing the cure for cancer.

100 → 100 "In my journey, I've found comfort in looking at the many people who are genuinely dedicated to fighting diseases and making the world a better place. Think about the doctors and nurses who work tirelessly, often in challenging conditions, to care for their patients…" 17 Evidence of extraterrestrial visitation is being suppressed (Greer, Howe). 60 → 68 "It's really understandable why the work of Stephen Greer, Linda Mouton Howe, and similar figures can be so captivating. They speak to the wonders of the universe and our natural desire to connect with something greater than ourselves… But when it comes to the specific idea that there's a cover-up regarding extraterrestrial life, I believe this notion doesn't hold up."

Note.

Selected from the 129 No Facts conversations in the Study 1 analytic sample. Excerpts are verbatim; "…" marks omitted text. Pre → Post: the participant's belief in the focal conspiracy (0 to 100) before and after the conversation.

S10.3 Word Counts by Condition

Table S34 gives the number of words the AI wrote per conversation (three turns summed) in each Study 1 condition. The Concise prompt asked for responses "as short as possible while still being persuasive" and set no numeric target. It roughly halved output (588 vs. 1,226 words in the Baseline treatment; 52% fewer) and cut extracted claims by about a third (17.7 vs. 26.2 per conversation; Table S24), so Concise messages were denser in claims. No Facts conversations were the second shortest (829 words) but carried far fewer claims (7.1 per conversation, 0.7 of them specific-empirical). Because No Facts was longer than Concise and its prompt also barred reasoning and alternative explanations, the two conditions differ in kind and do not bracket a minimum effective dose.

S11. Code and Data Availability

The data and the code behind the numbers, tables and figures in this supplement are in the replication materials; the README there names the few cached outputs with no producing script. A single R Markdown document, organized broadly by supplement section, runs or loads the analyses; knitting it produces a report that checks the values registered in its audit table against the values the code computes. Language-model outputs ship cached, and their prompts are in the code or the survey files. S7.1 Claim Extraction Pipeline

Each AI message about the focal belief in both studies was sent, in a separate call, to GPT-4.1 (OpenAI; February-March 2026). The system prompt said: "Extract only specific factual claims stated in the text, regardless of whether they are true or false. Do not include larger Moral/political share smaller ones; the other four shares were not significantly associated with belief change (Table S26).

S7.5 Alternative-Model Sensitivity

Because a share depends on the total number of claims, Table S26 also measures the focal type by its raw count, by its raw count controlling for the other five types, and by its logratio balance against the rest. The Moral/political and Meta-epistemic estimates are negative and the Inferential-logical estimates positive under all four specifications.

S7.6 Claim Composition by Focal-Conspiracy Veracity

To check whether claim composition depended on how true participants' focal conspiracies were, we labeled each focal belief (the AI's one-sentence restatement) with Perplexity Sonar Pro (a web-searching language model). The prompt asked it to judge the belief's central conspiratorial implication rather than its background premises, and to return a 0-100 veracity score and one of four labels: false or mostly false, mixed or partly true, plausible or mostly true, or too vague to check. Of the 1,290 Study 1 participants, 70.2% held a false or Both treatments reduced belief relative to Control. Reduction was larger at higher pretreatment belief (see §S4.5 on regression to the mean) and higher AI trust, and smaller at higher belief importance, more Republican party preference and greater social embeddedness. Age, religiosity, education and prior exposure to counterarguments showed no independent association.
